# SPEC — sim

Format: `- **ID** [principle][milestone] requirement — verification`

Kinds of verification: U = unit/property test, I = integration test (feed an event sequence to the core's App or to sim's state), E = terminal E2E (tmux), B = benchmark.
Test names start with the ID in lowercase (SAFE-1 → `safe_1_...`). scripts/trace.sh checks the mapping.
Sub-items that start with **Decided** record what was settled while implementing, with the principle that settled it.
They bind the implementation the same way as the line above them.

sim is one program in two crates, and a window in a third. **The core** (`sim-editor-core`, `core/`) is what keeps text from being lost: the buffer, vi's grammar, saves, the merge with what other processes write, the terminal. **The editor** (`sim-editor`, `sim/`) adds what living in a project takes: a tree, finding, several files, syntax, the looks. Part one is the core's and holds for every file open in sim and for `sim --plain`, which is the core alone (PLAIN-1); part two only adds. The principles P1 to P5 of part one are the core's (CONCEPT), those of part two the editor's; P1, safety, is the same. The milestones are three lines of work: `M` for the core, `S` for the editor and `G` for the window (`.milestone` holds the current one of each). **The window** (`sim-gui`, `gui/`) is part three: another binary, `sim-gui`, that shows the editor's screen as surfaces on a GPU instead of cells in a terminal; it is not published and is not in the `sim` binary or in the `sim-editor` crate's dependencies. What it needs of the editor is part two's seams, GUI-1 to GUI-7: small public APIs that the terminal's own code is also written on.

# Part one — the core

## Startup and exit

- **CLI-1** [P5][M1] `sim [+cmd] [file]`. `+N` goes to line N, `+/re` searches, and any other `+cmd` runs as an ex command. It runs after the first render — I
  - **Decided** (P5): `--` ends the options, so a file named `+x` can be opened. Any other argument that starts with `-`, or a second file, prints usage and exits with 2. Without a file, sim opens an unnamed buffer, and `:w {file}` gives it that name.
  - **Decided** (P1): an argument that is not valid UTF-8 (a file name from an old archive, in Latin-1 or Shift-JIS) prints `sim: a file name that is not valid UTF-8 cannot be opened` and exits with 2, like any argument sim cannot use. Paths are text throughout sim, and a lossy name would open another file; `std::env::args` would have panicked.
- **CLI-2** [P3][M1] `file:line[:col]` is accepted. But if a file whose name is exactly that string exists, that file is opened — U
- **CLI-3** [P5][M2] The exit code is 0 for `:q` `:wq` `:x`, and 1 for `:cq` (used to abort git commit) — I
  - **Decided** (P5): `:q!`, `ZZ`, `ZQ` and `q` exit with 0 as well, so `:cq` is the only way to fail on purpose. `git commit` with `GIT_EDITOR=sim` is checked end to end: `ZZ` commits and `:cq` aborts.
- **CLI-4** [P1][M1] Every way out, including panic, SIGTERM and SIGHUP, restores the terminal state — E
  - **Decided** (P1): SIGTERM and SIGHUP reach App as an event. Unsaved changes are snapshotted (SAFE-6) before exiting with 128 + the signal number.
  - **Decided** (P1): a failed write to the terminal counts as a hangup: it reaches App as SIGHUP, so the unsaved text is snapshotted and sim exits with 129, instead of leaving at once with 1.

- **CLI-5** [P2][M4] `sim --plain` without a file shows an intro until the first key, as Vim's does: what sim is, and the keys to type, save, quit and walk the changes. The view draws it; it never becomes text, and it doesn't show when recovered text fills the buffer — U (golden)
  - **Decided** (P2): the intro is plain text in the two achromatic levels: `sim {version}` (the Cargo version), the one line of CONCEPT, and the keys to type, save, quit and walk the changes (`i / Esc`, `Ctrl-s`, `ZZ`, `q`, `ZQ`, `]c / [c`), centered below the first row (which keeps the empty line 1 and the cursor). It is at most 12 rows; the lines that don't fit a short screen are dropped from the bottom, and a narrow one cuts them. It goes with the first key (or when text is in the buffer), and it is never part of the buffer.

- **CLI-6** [P2][M4] Reopening a file puts the cursor where it was when the file was last closed (remembered per file in the state directory, for the last 1,000 files), so reading continues where it stopped. Not with `+cmd` or `file:line`, and not for git's message files (`COMMIT_EDITMSG`, `MERGE_MSG`, `TAG_EDITMSG`, `git-rebase-todo`), which start at the top — I
  - From the author's nvim (a `BufReadPost` restore, as in vim's own defaults.vim): continuity of context.
  - **Decided** (P2): the place is the cursor's line and column (0-based). It is remembered by whatever closes the file: `:q` `:q!` `:wq` `:x` `:cq` `q` `ZZ` `ZQ`, a signal, and an `:e` (a reload too, which then puts the cursor back), and not by a quit that is refused. A buffer without a file remembers nothing.
  - **Decided** (P2): it comes back as an event after the file has loaded, and applies only to the file that is open, while the cursor has not moved, in Normal mode: the user who was quicker keeps their place. It is clamped to the text, centered (VIS-12), and applied to a read-only buffer too. The names of git's message files are matched as a whole file name (`.git/COMMIT_EDITMSG`, but not `COMMIT_EDITMSG.bak`).
  - **Decided** (P2): the key of a file is its canonical path (SAFE-6), the store is `positions` in the private state directory (SAFE-6), one `line<TAB>col<TAB>key` per line, least recently used first. The journal thread reads it again for each change, so sessions on different files keep one another's entries, and writes it atomically (a rename) but without waiting for the disk to sync it, since a place is not worth the wait at exit (PERF-1: with a sync `sim --plain +q` took 7 ms); what does not read as an entry is skipped, and a path with a line break in it loses its entry. The same thread answers the lookup for an `:e` (as an `Event::Place` after the file's `Opened`), so it comes after the place of the file that `:e` left; the first lookup is made at startup, before the first frame. Without a state directory nothing is kept between sessions.
## One program

- **PLAIN-1** [P1][M5] `sim --plain` is the core alone: the calm look (syntax colors stay: they run in the process, and `zh` turns them off), one file (an unnamed buffer without one), no panel, `:e name` replaces the file as it always did, and no program of its own is run: not git, not ripgrep, not a language server, not a filter (`:{range}!cmd` says `plain mode runs no program: a filter is refused`) and not the hook (VI-11). The status line says `plain`, and never the hint of ICON-1 (`zi` is refused there). It is the default when the effective uid is 0, and when sim is the editor of `sudoedit` (the parent process is `sudo`): there the file belongs to someone more privileged than the person at the keys, and an editor that runs programs on the strength of a directory or of the environment must not — U, I, E
  - **Decided** (P1): `--plain` may stand anywhere before a `--`. Nothing turns it off for root: plain mode takes the reason away instead of guarding each program one by one.
  - **Decided** (P1): the switch is one way and global (`filter::forbid_programs`, set before anything asks for a program) and every place that starts one asks it first: `filter::spawn` (filters and the hook), the editor's `git` and its content search. A caller added later cannot forget it, and the test that runs a fake `git` and `rg` first on the `PATH` (E2E `plain_1_...`) sees them never run, where sim without `--plain` runs them.
  - **Decided** (P1): plain mode holds for every front end, the terminal and the window (GUI-4) alike, because the start is one function and not each front end's own: `shell::begin` takes `--plain` or a privileged world (`World::here()`: the uid and the parent process, `PATH` and the state directory, as the system says them), sets `Env.plain`, runs `filter::forbid_programs` before anything can ask for a program, points git's hooks at a private empty directory only when it is not plain (GIT-1), and gives the shell no `rg` in plain mode. A front end brings only what it differs in (`Front`: its size, its colors, its look, its icons, its hook) and cannot start sim without the rules; `plain_1_the_terminal_starts_sim_only_through_shell_begin` and `gui_8_the_window_starts_sim_only_through_the_start_every_front_end_shares` scan the two for a start of their own, and the tests of `begin_plain.rs`, `gui/tests/plain_1.rs` and their controls (`begin_open.rs`, `gui/tests/begin_open.rs`) start in a privileged world and find no git, no filter, no hook and no `rg` run, and the control runs them.
  - **Decided** (P1): the detection makes no file and runs no program: the effective uid and the parent's pid come from `geteuid` and `getppid`, and the parent's name from `/proc/{ppid}/comm`. A first version asked a system without `/proc` (macOS) by creating `$TMPDIR/.sim-uid-{pid}` and reading its owner: a name anyone can guess, followed through a planted link and truncated (`File::create`), under `sudo` (which resets `TMPDIR` to `/tmp`) a way to truncate a file of root's, in the very case the detection is for. It is gone, and a test plants a link at that name and shows nothing touches it; no source outside `#[cfg(test)]` names the shared temporary directory (the tests use a private, create-new directory, and the scripts `mktemp`).
  - **Decided** (P1): the limits. On systems without `/proc` the journal asks `kill -0` (a program) whether the process of an old snapshot is alive; that is not run on the file's behalf and stays. `sudoedit` is recognised on Linux (the name of the parent in `/proc`); on macOS only the uid tells, and `SUDO_EDITOR='sim --plain'` is the way to say it there.
  - **Decided** (P2): `zl` says `plain mode: the look is calm` and changes nothing, `zi` does nothing, and the panel's commands `:qa` and `:wa` do not exist, as they did not in the program sim was folded from: the cases of the core (E2E, core/scripts/e2e.sh) run against `sim --plain`, except the filters and the hook, which run against sim.
- **STATE-1** [P1][M5] There is one state directory, `$XDG_STATE_HOME/sim` (else `$HOME/.local/state/sim`): snapshots, the recovery copies of SAFE-7 (`.disk`, `.yours`), the places, the history, the look and the icons. What `$XDG_STATE_HOME/si` holds (the name of the directory before sim was one program) is copied into it once, when sim starts, and the old directory is never written to or deleted from — U (journal), E
  - **Decided** (P1): copied, not read in place or moved: a recovery that reads two directories has two rules and two places to look in, and a move can lose files between two writes; a copy loses nothing and leaves the way back. What the new directory has already is kept as it is, a file that is not ours, not a regular file or a leftover `.tmp` is not copied, and a mark `.migrated` in the new directory says it was done, so that what is deleted from the new one does not come back. A copy that fails leaves no mark and is tried again; the failure is told on the status line. The old directory is only read if it is private (SAFE-6 held for it as for the new one).
  - **Decided** (P1): the format of a snapshot keeps its magic word, `si-snapshot 1`: the snapshots the old directory held are read as they are.
  - **Decided** (P1): everything sim keeps in the directory follows SAFE-6's rule, not only the journal: a directory that is not private, or is a link, is not used, and is not made private either (what is in it may have been readable); the history, the look and the icons say so on the status line (`icons: /path is not private (yours, mode 700)`) and the snapshots with `cannot write snapshots: ...`.

## Safety

- **SAFE-1** [P1][M1] Saving creates a temporary file in the same directory with 0600 and O_EXCL → writes it → fsync → rename → fsync of the directory, in this order. Whichever step fails, either the old or the new version remains intact — U (fault injection at each step)
  - **Decided** (P1): before the rename, the temporary file gets the original's mode and group. If the group cannot be set, the in-place path of SAFE-2 is used instead.
  - **Decided** (P1): a symlink is saved through to its target, and the link itself stays.
  - **Decided** (P1, P5): a file that does not exist yet gets the mode any new file would get, so its temporary file is created with 0666 reduced by the umask instead of 0600. This exposes nothing the final file doesn't.
  - **Decided** (P3): saving runs on a background thread, and editing continues meanwhile. `:wq` and `:x` quit only after the save has succeeded.
  - **Decided** (P1): once the rename has happened the save counts as done, even if syncing the directory then fails (some systems refuse it); the status line says so as a warning.
  - **Decided** (P1): a dangling symlink is followed to its missing target, which is created; the link stays. A file without write permission is not saved over: `read-only file (:w! to write anyway)`; `:w!` writes it and keeps its mode.
  - **Decided** (P1): sim writes through a symlink, dangling or not, only if the user or root owns the link; a link owned by anybody else (a planted link in a shared directory such as /tmp) is refused with `not saved: x is a symlink owned by someone else`, like Linux's `fs.protected_symlinks`.
  - **Decided** (P1): "the user" is our effective uid, read from the system (`file::euid`, rustix's `geteuid`): not the owner of the file's directory (a directory another user owns may be one we can write to, and a link planted in it is theirs) and not the owner of a file made there to see (that is the filesystem's choice on a mount that maps owners, and it cost a file made and removed in the directory on every save through a link). `file::save` is `save_by(euid, ...)`; the tests give `save_by` another uid, and as root make a directory and a link of another user's.
  - **Decided** (P1): that holds for every symlink met while resolving the path, directory links included: a path that passes through someone else's link is refused, not only one whose last component is a link.
  - **Decided** (P1): an in-place save (SAFE-2) opens the file without following a final symlink (`O_NOFOLLOW`, set through `custom_flags` with the platform's constant) and checks through the opened handle that it is the same file (device and inode) that was validated, so a file swapped between the check and the write is refused rather than written through.
  - **Decided** (P1): the path is walked one component at a time, as the kernel does: a link's target is spliced in where the link was, and `..` is the parent of where the links before it lead (not of where they are). Each link met is looked at once, those reached through other links included, and the first one that neither the user nor root owns refuses the save with its absolute path (the path's own final link keeps the name it was given). At most 40 links are followed, like the kernel: a loop is no path, so the save fails with the system's `Too many levels of symbolic links` and nothing is written or made. The user's uid is asked (the owner of a probe file, as before) only when the path has a link, so a save without one creates nothing extra. Links owned by root (uid 0) are trusted: the system's own, such as macOS's `/tmp`, `/var` (which `$TMPDIR` lies under) and `/etc`, or Arch's `/bin`; a link someone planted is owned by whoever planted it, as with `fs.protected_symlinks`.
- **SAFE-2** [P1][M1] If the file is owned by someone else, has two or more hard links, or its directory is not writable, save in place. Before that, write a recovery copy and fsync it; delete it once the save has succeeded — U
  - **Decided** (P1): the recovery copy goes to `$XDG_STATE_HOME/sim/` (0700), because the file's own directory may not be writable. If the in-place write fails, the status line names the recovery copy.
  - **Decided** (P1): between looking at the file and writing it, the file may be swapped (a link to another file of the user's, or a different file renamed over it). It is opened for writing without following a final link (`O_NOFOLLOW`, whose value is the kernel's for Linux on x86 and ARM and macOS' `0x100`; elsewhere 0, and the check below alone holds) and without truncating, its (device, inode) is compared with those of the file that was validated, and only then is it truncated and written. A file that is not the validated one is not touched: `not saved: <path> changed while saving`. Nothing was written, so the recovery copy is removed, as for the other refusals before the write. The atomic save needs no such check: it creates its temporary file with `O_EXCL` and `rename` replaces the name without following it, so a swapped name is replaced (SAFE-5 compares the content first) and nothing is written through it.
  - **Decided** (P1): a file that is a mount point (a file bind-mounted into a container, such as a mounted config) cannot be replaced by a rename: the system answers EBUSY, and every `:w` and `:w!` failed for good with `Device or resource busy`. That answer to the rename sends the save down the in-place path, with its recovery copy, like the other files that cannot be replaced.
- **SAFE-3** [P1][M1] Opening and saving without editing gives byte-identical output (line endings, whether there is a final newline, BOM, permissions) — U (property test)
  - **Decided** (P1): if every line break is CRLF, the buffer holds LF and saving writes CRLF. Any other mix is kept exactly as it is (`\r` shows as `^M`, SAFE-8).
  - **Decided** (P1, P2): an empty or new file gets a final newline once it has text, since a text file ends with one (git and POSIX tools expect it). An existing file keeps whatever it had.
  - **Decided** (P1, P5): deleting all the text of a file and saving writes an empty file (0 bytes), as vim does; undo brings back the final-newline state with the text.
  - **Decided** (P1): text that goes and comes back inside one change (`ccxyz`) is not an emptied file: with text again the file ends as it did, so one that had no final newline has none. A file that was saved empty stays an empty file until it has text, and then ends as the file it was.
- **SAFE-4** [P1][M1] A file that is not valid UTF-8, or has a NUL in its first 8 KB, opens read-only and shows why — U
  - **Decided** (P1): invalid bytes are shown as U+FFFD. Edits and every kind of write are refused, because the text on screen is not the file. A file that cannot be read (no permission, a directory) opens the same way with an empty buffer.
  - **Decided** (P2): a directory says `<path> is a directory` (finding files is the editor's job, TREE-1).
  - **Decided** (P2): the status line says `read-only: {path} is a directory`, or `read-only: cannot read: {why}`.
- **SAFE-5** [P1][M3] Right before saving, the hash of the content on disk is compared with the hash at load (the base). If they differ, nothing is written: the disk content goes to the merge of DISK-2, and what that cannot take in stays on disk, so both versions are kept (SAFE-7) — I
  - **Decided** (P1): the check runs on the save thread right before the rename (compare-and-swap). After the merge, nothing is saved automatically; the status line says the file was not saved, and `:w` saves.
  - **Decided** (P1): the message is `not saved: the file changed on disk; merged its changes (:w to save)`, or with conflicts `not saved: the file changed on disk and conflicts with yours: {SAFE-7's two ways out}`. The disk content goes through exactly the merge of a watched change (DISK-2), so the two ways in give the same buffer. A `:wq`, `:x` or `ZZ` that meets a conflict does not quit; the next one is checked against what was merged. If the file is gone, nothing is merged: the status line says `not saved: the file was deleted on disk (:w to create it again)`, and the next `:w` creates it (DISK-1). A conflict on `:w {other}` is not a merge (the content is another file's): `not saved: {other} exists (:w! to overwrite)`.
  - **Decided** (P1): right before the rename (or the truncate of an in-place save) the file's stamp (device, inode, size, mtime, ctime) is checked again, and its hash only if the stamp moved. A write in the same microsecond, or a same-size rewrite within one timestamp tick of a coarse file system, can still slip through.
  - **Decided** (P1, P3): the compare reads the file, so a target that is not a regular file (a FIFO that took the file's place would wait for a writer for ever, and the save with it; a device may never end) is refused with `not saved: {path} is not a regular file` before it is read. `:w!` compares nothing and replaces the name as before (what it keeps of the disk's version is SAFE-7's).
- **SAFE-6** [P1][M2] Unsaved changes are snapshotted at most once per second into `$XDG_STATE_HOME/sim/` (0700). Opening the same file after an abnormal exit offers to restore them. At most one second of work is lost — I
  - **Decided** (P1): a snapshot holds the path, the base text and the buffer text, in a file (0600) whose name includes the process id, so two sessions on one file don't overwrite each other's snapshots. A background thread writes it (P3). It is deleted after a successful save and on quitting with `:q`, `:q!` or `:cq`; it stays after a crash or a signal. A snapshot whose process is still running is left alone.
  - **Decided** (P5): if the disk still matches the snapshot's base, restoring applies the snapshot text as one undo step, and the status line says how old they are, e.g. `recovered unsaved changes from 3 minutes ago · u to undo` (an age needs no time zone, which sim cannot read without libc). If the disk has changed since, the snapshot goes through the merge of DISK-2 as `mine` (base: the snapshot's base, theirs: the disk), as one undo step, and the status line says `recovered changes from 3 minutes ago, merged with disk · u to undo`; what conflicts is marked and the buffer is diverged (DISK-2, SAFE-7); the snapshot's text stays in the buffer.
  - **Decided** (P1): the shell sends a tick at least every second, from a timer thread, so continuous typing is snapshotted too. On a tick, if the buffer is modified, its version has changed since the last snapshot and 1000 ms of tick time have passed since the last one, the App asks for a snapshot (`Effect::Snapshot`, holding the path and ropes, which are O(1) clones; the journal thread turns them into bytes). Tick times are 1000 ms apart by construction, so at most one second of work is lost. The core never reads a clock; the journal thread stamps the snapshot's time, and the shell passes the age of a recovered one in ms.
  - **Decided** (P1): the App asks the journal to forget (`Effect::Unsnapshot`) whenever the buffer becomes clean (saved, or undone to the saved text, or `:e!`) and before every `Quit` that a key or a save causes: `:q`, `:q!`, `:cq`, `ZQ`, `q`, and a successful `:wq` `:x` `ZZ`. A signal asks for a snapshot of the unsaved text, whatever the clock says, and keeps it.
  - **Decided** (P1): an unnamed buffer is protected too. Its journal key is the empty path, so `sim` without a file offers the unsaved text of a crashed unnamed session. The key of a file is its canonical path, or the absolute path for a file that does not exist yet (symlinks are followed, as in SAFE-1). The App sends the path with every snapshot, because only it knows whether `:w {file}` has named the buffer or a `:e` was refused.
  - **Decided** (P3): one journal thread runs snapshots and removals in order, so a key never waits for a disk. Before sim exits (`Quit`, signal, SIGHUP) the shell closes its queue and waits for that thread for at most 2 seconds, so a SIGTERM right after an edit still leaves the last snapshot.
  - **Decided** (P1): a recovered snapshot belongs to a dead process. It is deleted only once the buffer's own snapshot (which then holds the same text or newer) is on disk, or when the buffer is clean again or we quit. A snapshot that was not used (it is not applied to a read-only buffer) is never deleted by sim; one that was merged is handled like an applied one.
  - **Decided** (P1): recovery runs at startup before a `+cmd`, so that command works on the recovered text (and, as with any key, clears the status message). It is not applied to a read-only buffer.
  - **Decided** (P1): a snapshot that cannot be used because the buffer is read-only is kept and left alone, and every open of that file says `kept unsaved changes from {age}, not applied: {file}`, with the snapshot's own file name.
  - **Decided** (P1): a snapshot that cannot be written is told once, on the status line: `cannot write snapshots: {why}`. The journal thread sends the first failure as an event (`Notice`) and stays quiet afterwards, since the next ticks would only say it again.
  - **Decided** (P1): the state directory must be private: it is used only if it is a real directory (not a symlink) owned by the user with no access for group or others, and snapshot files not owned by the user are ignored. Without `$XDG_STATE_HOME` and `$HOME` (or with relative paths) there is no state directory, never a shared fallback such as /tmp; snapshots and recovery copies are then off, and the status line says so once.
  - **Decided** (P1): a snapshot of a file that no longer exists is restored whole (its absence is not a deletion of every line), and the buffer counts as modified.
- **SAFE-7** [P1][M3] While the disk holds changes that conflict with mine, `:w` is refused and nothing is written over them. Both versions are kept, and each of the two explicit ways out copies the version it gives up into the state directory first: `:w!` keeps mine (after copying the disk's), `:e!` takes the disk's (after copying mine) — I, E
  - **Decided** (P1): the owner's call, 2026-10-17, which replaces the proposals of the first design (a conflict was a hunk that `y` and `n` decided, and the disk's text lived in the buffer until then): a conflict needs no concept of its own. Mine stays in the buffer, its lines marked where the disk differs (DISK-2), and what the disk holds stays on the disk. The buffer is then diverged: `App` keeps the disk's last version (`theirs`) until `:w!`, `:e!` or a load ends it, or the file comes to hold what the buffer holds. Nothing of either side is lost on the way, and nothing has to be decided hunk by hunk.
  - **Decided** (P1): `:w`, `:wq`, `:x`, `ZZ` and Ctrl-s (in Insert mode too, which stays Insert mode) are refused while the buffer is diverged, with `not saved: the file on disk conflicts with yours: :w! keeps yours (the disk's goes to the state directory), :e! takes the disk's (yours goes there)`, and write nothing. `:w {other}` is allowed: saving mine under another name is how to keep it. `:q` is refused only as vi refuses it (modified, `:q!` discards): what the disk holds is on the disk and needs nothing from the buffer.
  - **Decided** (P1): `:w!` first asks the shell to copy the disk's version (`Effect::Keep`: the bytes the buffer has of it) and writes only when the copy is done (`Event::Kept`). The copy is a file of the state directory (0600, synced, named after the file and ending `.disk`, never deleted by sim), so a crash between the copy and the write leaves the disk's version on the disk and in the copy, and mine in the snapshot of SAFE-6: nothing is lost either way. If the copy cannot be made (no state directory, a full disk) nothing is written: `not saved: cannot keep the disk's version: {why}`. The save then says `saved {file}; the disk's version is in {path}`, and that stays on the status line until the next key: it is where the path of the kept copy is told (the path as a shell takes it, below the home directory `~/...` as UI-2's header shows a root, else absolute, and `<Space>bk` says it again: see below). Once the save is done mine is the file, and the marks of the disk's changes, the conflict and the changes-on-disk badges, and the tab's mark go with the conflict (a reload by `:e!` clears them the same way), because the disk no longer differs from the buffer. The conflict ends when the save is done, not when it is asked for: a save that fails ends nothing, and anything the disk says in the meantime is read again after the answer (DISK-1).
  - **Decided** (P1): `:e!` while the buffer is diverged copies mine the same way (a file ending `.yours`) and reloads only when the copy is done; if it cannot be made, the buffer is as it was: `not reloaded: cannot keep yours: {why}`. The load then says `loaded {file}; yours is in {path}` (the path told the same way). Without a conflict `:e!` discards the changes as vi does. `:q!`, `ZQ` and `:cq` discard mine as always, and the disk's version is where it was.
  - **Decided** (P1): the disk's content that cannot be merged (it is no longer text, DISK-2) counts as a conflict: the bytes are kept the same way, so a `:w!` over a file that became binary keeps it first.
  - **Decided** (P1): where a copy went must stay findable whatever the state directory is and however wide the screen. CI on macOS (2026-10-18) showed a message that ended in the copy's absolute path cut by the line: `$TMPDIR` there is `/var/folders/xx/.../T/`, and a real state directory is `/home/user/.local/state/sim/` before the name, 38 columns of words and 45 of path do not fit 80. So the path is told as a shell takes it, `~/.local/state/sim/a.rs.1.disk` below the home directory (`sim::tilde`, UI-2's rule; the core is told this string in `Event::Kept` and puts it in its message as it was, nothing of the core changed), and `<Space>bk` says it again whenever it is asked, as `<Space>gi` does for the guard (GIT-5): the path of the last copy made by any file's `:w!` or `:e!`, alone on the status line, so that all of the width is its own; a path wider than the line is cut from the left (`…`, `layout::tail`), because the file's name is what tells the copies apart and the directory is the state directory's, which `$XDG_STATE_HOME` says. Before any copy it says `no other version was kept`. A one-row line cannot show a path longer than itself in full: that is the one case where the whole path is not on the screen, and the copy is in the state directory all the same (STATE-1) with the name it has. The key is `<Space>bk` and not a git key: the copy is a file's, not the repository's.
- **SAFE-8** [P1][M1] Control characters (C0, C1, DEL) are shown in `^[` form. Bidirectional controls (U+200E–200F, U+202A–202E, U+2066–2069), U+200B, and U+FEFF anywhere but the start are shown in `<U+202E>` form. None of them is ever sent raw to the terminal (against Trojan Source) — U
  - **Decided** (P2): caret notation has no form for C1, so C1 (U+0080–U+009F) uses the `<U+0085>` form. A tab is shown as spaces (VIS-8).
  - **Decided** (P1): U+061C (Arabic letter mark) is a bidirectional control too, shown the same way.
  - **Decided** (P1): a char shown in another form is not told from the same text typed in the file: `Style::Hidden` is a span of the Frame's rows over each such token (`^[`, `<U+202E>`), whatever else the char's style is: a right-to-left override that someone else has just written into the file is a char of an outside change, and that is the Trojan Source case the style is for (a first version gave the style only to primary chars, and the attacker's own change hid it: found by a security review, 2026-10-17). The change around it still shows on the chars beside it and in the gutter. `Term` draws it in reverse video, bold with no palette, in the primary color with one. The literal text `<U+202E>` in a file is ordinary chars and has none. The status and command lines, whose tokens are not a file's chars, have none. The reader of a file sees at once which chars are not what they seem (sim draws them as the alert's block, UI-15). The change cost three lines of `src/`, and the limit moved from 3,600 to 3,603 for them, with the owner's agreement of 2026-10-17 that limits move for a stated reason; the removal of the proposals the same day took it down to what is left, 3,425.
  - **Decided** (P1): the interface's own words are made of what files hold (a directory's name, a heading, a definition's name), and are drawn through `layout::fit` and `Line::put` like every other row text, so a `\x1b]0;x\x07` or a `\x1b[2J` in them is U+FFFD on the screen and never reaches the terminal (2026-10-18: the breadcrumb of flat's tab bar was appended raw, found in review; UI-18).
- **SAFE-9** [P1][M1] Settings and commands are never read from file contents (no modelines). No network access. Tests check that core/src and sim/src have no `std::net`, and that the core's dependencies match its allowlist (scripts/check.sh reads what cargo resolved) — U
  - **Decided** (P1): the allowlist is the core's: ropey, crossterm, notify, rustix, similar, unicode-width and regex-lite. `signal-hook` was on it while the core had a program of its own (CLI-4 and SAFE-6 need SIGTERM and SIGHUP without `unsafe`); the signals are the shell's, so it is the editor's dependency now (crossterm depends on it already, so the binary doesn't grow). `rustix` (its `process` feature only) is there for `geteuid` and `getppid`: who runs sim, from the system, not from a file made to see who owns it (SAFE-1, SAFE-6, PLAIN-1). It is in the tree already, at the same version, through crossterm, so no crate is added and `unsafe_code = "forbid"` stands. The editor adds syntect and two-face for SYN-1, and nothing else is added without the reason written here.

## External changes

- **DISK-1** [P1,P2][M3] Watch the parent directory of the open file and take in changes on disk within 100 ms (including saves by rename, deletion and re-creation). Events caused by my own saves are recognized by their hash and ignored — I
  - **Decided** (P1): when the file is deleted, the buffer keeps its text and counts as modified, and the status line says `deleted on disk` (as a note next to `modified`, until the file is saved or comes back). The next `:w` expects no file, so it creates it. If the file comes back from outside, its content is merged like any change on disk.
  - **Decided** (P1): our own save is recognized by the hash of the bytes that were written: the one in flight (the watcher may be quicker than the save thread's answer) and, once it is done, the one of the file as last read or written. Both are ignored, and so is any event whose content hashes like what the buffer already knows. A buffer without a file name is not watched.
  - **Decided** (P1): the App asks for the watch (`Effect::Watch(path)`) whenever it gets a file name (a `:e` that loaded, or the first `:w {file}` of an unnamed buffer); the shell watches the resolved path (symlinks followed), one file at a time, and starts watching before it reads the file at startup so that no change slips in between.
  - **Decided** (P1): the order of a watcher read and a save's answer can't be known, so after every save's answer the file is read once more and taken in like any change (an unchanged file is ignored by its hash). A change seen while the save was in flight is never dropped.
  - **Decided** (P3): that read is the first act of the new watcher's own thread, which also does the debounced reads, so it does not overtake a later read; it reaches App as a normal `Disk` event. The thread also makes the hash of the content (`Disk { hash }`), because hashing 10 MB takes 8 ms (reading it 1 ms) and no key should wait for that; a `Disk` without a hash is hashed by App.
  - **Decided** (P1): every read of the disk is numbered by the shell, from one counter shared by all its threads: the watcher's reads (the first read of a new watcher, which follows every save's answer, too), the read for an `:e`, and the answer of a save (`Saved` and `SaveConflict`, whose compare is a read). A read takes its number as it starts; the answer of a save takes it when the save is done, since a read that began before the rename may still show the old file. App keeps the newest number it has taken in (a load, a save's answer, a read) and drops a `Disk` older than that: the watcher can be held up between reading and sending for as long as the keys take, and then its read was taken for a change. (Found by the model, about 1 case in 100,000: a read taken before an `:e!` load arrived after the load and a `:wq`, and the next save lost the outside words.) A `SaveConflict` older than that merges nothing (`not saved: the file changed on disk meanwhile (:w to save again)`), an `Opened` of the same file older than that is refused (`not opened: the file changed meanwhile (:e! again)`), and a `Saved` is never dropped, since the save happened: the fresh read that follows every answer says what the disk holds now. A load of another file (`:e other`) is not ordered against reads of this one.
  - **Decided** (P1): what the watcher of the file being left reads must not be merged into the file that replaces it: a `Disk` carries no file name, and a read begun after the load's number was taken would pass for news of the new file (found with a file rewritten every few milliseconds while `:e other` ran: `other` showed the other file's text, marked as changed on disk). So when the shell begins the read of an `:e` it first silences the running watcher (a flag its reports check) and only then takes the number: what the watcher had sent is ahead of the answer in the queue, and what it read but could not send began earlier than the answer, which App drops by its number. The App asks for a watch after every answer to an `:e`, one it refuses too, since the shell silenced the old watcher either way; that watcher's first read covers the time in between.
  - **Decided** (P2): a watch can die without a word: the directory it watches is deleted and made again (`rm -rf dir && …`, `git checkout`), and the new directory is not the one the kernel knew; an event queue can overflow; some file systems send no events. Then the file would stay `deleted on disk` or old for ever. So the watcher also looks at the file's stamp (device, inode, size, mtime, ctime) once a second, and a file that is not as the last read saw it is read and taken in like any change, within a second. A file that does not change costs one `stat` a second.
- **DISK-2** [P1,P2][M3] If the buffer is clean, reload it and mark what changed. If it is dirty, run a 3-way merge of base, mine and disk: take in the changes that don't conflict and mark them. Where both sides changed the same lines mine stays, its lines are marked, and the buffer is diverged (SAFE-7). The cursor keeps pointing at the same text — I
  - **Decided** (P1): a reload or a merge is one undo step. Afterwards, the disk content is the new base.
  - **Decided** (P1): the text and the marks (DISK-6) are both part of an undo step, so undoing a merge takes its marks away, redo brings them back, and undoing a reload leaves a buffer that differs from the file, so it counts as modified. The buffer is clean afterwards if and only if its text is the file's. Whether it is diverged is not part of the step: undoing a merge brings the old text back, but the disk still holds what it held, so the way out is still SAFE-7's.
  - **Decided** (P1): the three texts of the merge all end with a line break, so that a line added at the end is an added line and not a change of the last one. The file's format (BOM, CRLF, final newline) is the one on disk from then on, since that is what a save would replace.
  - **Decided** (P1): the cursor keeps pointing at the same text: its line is carried through the lines that the disk's changes replaced, and its column is kept as far as the line goes. Marks that exist already are carried the same way: a mark on a line the disk replaced is replaced by the new mark.
  - **Decided** (P1): a merge that brings in a different file leaves no older state clean: undoing past it gives a buffer that differs from the file, so it counts as modified. A merge of the file the base already was (recovery) can be undone to clean.
  - **Decided** (P1): a change that cannot be merged is not merged: when the buffer is read-only, or the file is no longer text (SAFE-4), the buffer stays as it is, the status line says `changed on disk, not as text: not merged (:e! reloads, :w! overwrites)`, and the file is not taken as the base, so a save is still refused (SAFE-5); the bytes are kept for SAFE-7 as a conflict.
  - **Decided** (P1): a conflict leaves mine in the buffer with a mark on each of its lines (`~`, the whole line: there is no word diff to show, since the disk's words are not in the buffer; a deletion point `-` on the line after it where mine has no line), and `App` keeps the bytes of the disk's version (`theirs`) for SAFE-7. A newer read of the file replaces them (only the last version is kept: the other process replaced its earlier one itself), and a read that makes the buffer equal to the file ends the conflict. A Visual selection ends when a merge comes in, since its anchor is a place in the old text; `'<` and `'>` follow their lines through the merge like the cursor does.
  - **Decided** (P1): undo across a merge that changed the base never makes the buffer clean, since the text then differs from the file. A file that changed only in its format (BOM, CRLF, final newline) counts as a changed base: what a save would write is not what the file holds.
  - **Decided** (P1): a finished save gives the buffer the format it wrote (BOM, CRLF, final newline), whatever a merge took in meanwhile, since the file has that format now. A buffer emptied meanwhile still writes an empty file (SAFE-3).
  - **Decided** (P2): the view follows a merge like the cursor: the first line on the screen keeps its text, so lines that come in or go above it do not make the screen jump.
  - **Decided** (P2): content that App has taken in already (the watcher's read arrives before the answer of the save that found it) is not merged again, so it makes no undo step that does nothing.
- **DISK-3** [P1][M3] The 3-way merge is our own, built from two `similar` line diffs. Conflict-marker strings are neither generated nor parsed — U
- **DISK-4** [P1][M3] Property: what a merge makes is mine with the changes of the disk that do not touch mine's. Where they overlap mine stays, and no line of either side that the other left alone is lost — U (property test, 10,000 cases)
  - **Decided** (P1): precisely, for a merge of base, mine and theirs: the text is mine plus the theirs changes that did not conflict (just mine when theirs is the base, just theirs when mine is the base, mine when the two are equal); every line of mine is in it, and every line of theirs that was changed without touching a change of mine's is too; what is left out of theirs is exactly the conflicts, which are reported as the lines of mine they overlap. The conflicts are what SAFE-7 keeps on the disk.
- **DISK-5** [P3][M3] Diffing gives up after 50 ms (the deadline of similar). Even on a 100,000-line file, taking in a change finishes within 50 ms — B
  - **Decided** (P3): the core reads no clock, so the shell stamps the instant with each event that makes a diff (`Disk`, `SaveConflict`, `Recover`): the time it read the content plus 50 ms. Past it a diff is coarse, never wrong: what it could not match counts as changed.
  - **Decided** (P3): word diffs (VIS-2) cost more than their lines are worth on a big rewrite, so one merge looks at 256 KiB of text for words at most. Past that, the lines of a change are painted whole and a mark has no word diff (every char of it counts as changed). Measured on 100,000 lines (release): a clean reload, a merge, a rewrite of every line and a conflict over the whole file each take about 20 ms through `App::update`, a frame included. The worst inputs for a diff (every line moved, or the same few lines everywhere) take about 55 ms: the diff gives up at 50 and the rest is linear.
- **DISK-6** [P2,P5][M3] A mark stays until the cursor enters its line. `:seen` clears all of them. `]c` and `[c` move to the next and the previous mark — I
  - **Decided** (P2): `]c` and `[c` go to the nearest mark in their direction and put the cursor on its first non-blank, centered (VIS-12). A mark on the cursor's own line is not the next one (the cursor has seen it). They stop at the ends (`no next change`, `no previous change`), ignore a count (P4), and a `]` or `[` that `c` does not follow does nothing.
  - **Decided** (P2): there is one mark per line of the buffer, with the symbol `+` (added), `~` (changed) or `-` (something was deleted just before the line, or after the last line), and the columns of the changed words in it. After every key, the mark of the cursor's line is gone: a motion, a search and a jump all count. A change that arrives while the cursor is on its line keeps its mark until the next key, so that it is seen at least once on the screen.
  - **Decided** (P2): an edit that changes a line takes its mark; lines moved by an edit above them keep theirs. A line that the edit leaves as it was (the one above `o`, the one below `dd`) is not changed. `:seen` clears marks only: the conflict with the disk (SAFE-7) stays until it is settled.

  - **Decided** (P2, P4): undo brings back the marks as they were before the undone step, including ones already seen; marks have no identity to remember them by, and showing a change again errs on the safe side. (A list of more than 1,024 marks is the exception, see PERF-2.)
- **FILT-1** [P3][M4] Filters (`:{range}!cmd`, `!` in Visual mode) run asynchronously with `sh -c`. You can keep working while one runs, and the status line shows the elapsed seconds. Ctrl-c cancels it — I
  - **Decided** (P4): one filter at a time. Ctrl-c stops the filter's whole process group.
  - **Decided** (P5): `:!cmd` without a range filters the current line, as `:s` does, and an empty command is `usage: :{range}!cmd`. The command gets the lines of the range, each followed by a line break (the last one too). A second filter is refused with `a filter is already running (Ctrl-c cancels it)`, and a filter is refused on a read-only buffer, where its result could not be applied.
  - **Decided** (P1): the buffer under a running filter is never replaced: an `:e` that loads meanwhile is refused with `not opened: a filter is running`. Quitting is not held up: the shell stops the filter then, since nobody is left to read its answer.
  - **Decided** (P3): the status line says `filter 3s · Ctrl-c cancel` after the other notes (VIS-9), counted by the ticks of the shell, which also redraw it, so it can be a second behind. Ctrl-c cancels in Normal mode with `cancelling the filter`; the answer then says `filter cancelled`, and a result that arrives anyway is not taken. In any other mode Ctrl-c does what it always did (Insert, Visual and the command line end). The shell stops the process group off the main thread (`kill` takes a few ms), and a command that cannot be started comes back as an exit code of 127 with the reason in its stderr.
  - **Decided** (P1, P3): the filter ends when its shell has, not when its output does: a child the shell left behind (a daemon a command started, or one that escaped the process group) can hold stdout and stderr open for ever, and then the filter would never end, Ctrl-c could not end it, and `:e` would be refused for good. The pipes are read as the shell runs, a chunk at a time; once it is gone, the output may take one second more to end, and what was read until then is the result.
- **FILT-2** [P1,P2][M4] When the filter ends, its output takes the place of the lines it was given, as one undo step (the edits I made meanwhile are kept; where they overlap, the output is not applied). With a nonzero exit code, the result is discarded and stderr is shown — I
  - **Decided** (P2): the status line shows the first non-blank line of stderr, or `filter failed (exit 3)` (`killed` for a signal) when there is none.
  - **Decided** (P1): the output is used only if it is text: invalid UTF-8 or a NUL byte discards it with `filter output is not text: discarded`, since a result made of U+FFFD would put text in the file that nobody wrote. An output that does not end with a line break gets one, so it cannot run into the line after the range. Empty output is a result (the lines are deleted). An output equal to the input says `no changes from the filter`.
  - **Decided** (P1, P2): the result is the merge (DISK-3) of the buffer at the filter's start (the base), the buffer as it is now (mine) and the text with the output in place of the range (theirs); it goes in as one undo step, replacing only the lines that differ, so the marks of changes from disk elsewhere stay, and the buffer is modified. `u` takes it back. Edits made meanwhile are kept. Where an edit overlaps the range the output is not applied at all, and nothing is lost: `filter result discarded: the lines changed meanwhile (run it again)`. The lines it changed are not marked: the user asked for them.
## Look

- **VIS-1** [P2][M1] No syntax highlighting. Text uses only two achromatic levels: primary (|Lc| 90) and secondary (|Lc| 60: line numbers) — U
- **VIS-2** [P2][M3] The single chromatic color, "change" (OKLCH h=250), is used only as a background. Changes are painted word by word (a run of letters, digits and `_` is one word; so is each CJK character, and each symbol) — U
- **VIS-3** [P2][M3] Changes are shown without color as well: the gutter shows `+` (added), `~` (changed) and `-` (where something was deleted) — U (golden)
  - **Decided** (P2): the symbol is the first column of the gutter and has the change style itself, so a column of changes can be seen without reading. A deletion point marks the line after it (the last line when the deletion was at the end).
  - **Decided** (P2): the words of a changed block are found by one word diff over the whole block (old lines against new lines), so a block of many lines is painted as the diff sees it, not line against line.
- **VIS-4** [P2][M5] Colors are computed with APCA-W3 0.0.98G-4g from the terminal's background, obtained with OSC 11 (waiting at most 3 ms). Primary text at |Lc| 90, secondary at |Lc| 60, the change background against the normal background at |Lc| ≥ 15, and text on the change background at |Lc| ≥ 75. With no reply, reverse (SGR 7) and faint (SGR 2) stand in; if the reply arrives late, redraw. Checked on every background of `#000000 #1e1e1e #282c34 #002b36 #ffffff #fafafa #fdf6e3 #eff1f5` — U
  - **Decided** (P2): primary and secondary are grays within ±1 of their target Lc. The Lc between the two backgrounds is computed with the change background as the text color.
  - **Decided** (P2): the three numbers cannot hold with one primary gray: 15 + 75 already uses up the 90 between primary and the background once APCA's offset is paid twice (measured: at most 68–72 on the eight backgrounds). So text on the change background gets its own gray, the one closest to primary that keeps |Lc| ≥ 75 there. It stays achromatic (VIS-1): the primary level adjusted to its background, not a new level of meaning.
  - **Decided** (P2): when no gray on primary's side reaches 75 on the change background (a mid-luminance terminal background, whose change color is light), that gray is black or white, and the other of the two is taken instead if it reads better there: on an orange background, white text on the light blue change was |Lc| 30 and black on it 76. Of the grid of backgrounds in steps of 5 per channel, the text on the change background was under |Lc| 60 on 29% before this and under 40 on 6%; after it, none is under 40.
  - **Decided** (P2): the query (`ESC ] 11 ; ? ESC \`) goes out right after entering the terminal (unless the startup keys quit, see below), and the first frame waits for the answer until 3 ms after that, ending the wait at once when it comes. Whatever else comes meanwhile (a key, a read of the disk) is kept and taken in order. The answer reaches sim as keys (crossterm reads `ESC ]` as Alt+`]`, its text as chars, and `ESC \` or BEL as Alt+`\` or Ctrl-g): the input thread puts it together into `Event::Bg` and none of its keys reaches App (VI-9's Alt+key rule does not apply to it). Only a text that starts with `11;` is held for it; any other key after Alt+`]` (and a text of 40 or more chars) goes on as keys in order, so a typed Esc and `]` are not lost, and an answer whose color cannot be read is swallowed. (Where the Alt+key rule is needed, in tmux and over ssh, a typed Esc and `]` that arrive in one read wait for the next key to be told from an answer, and are then given to App together with it.) The answer is taken whenever it comes, so a slow link still gets its colors; it is the whole reason the first frame does not wait for it any longer than 3 ms.
  - **Decided** (P2): App computes `color::palette(bg)` when the color comes (one `Event::Bg`, however late) and the Frame carries it. With a palette Term sends truecolor SGR for the four styles: Primary the primary gray, Secondary the secondary gray, Change the change background with the change text gray on it, Hidden (SAFE-8) the primary gray in reverse video; the Visual underline is unchanged, and the terminal's own background is never set. When the color comes late, everything is drawn again (one synchronized update); without an answer the reverse and faint stand-ins are used for as long as sim runs.
  - **Decided** (P1, P3): the query is sent after the screen is switched, and not at all when the keys given at startup already quit (`sim +q`): its answer would come after sim had gone, and the shell would type it. When it was sent and no color has come by the time of a quit, the quit waits for the terminal to be done answering, at most 100 ms, and only then gives the terminal back. A terminal answers in order, so the answer to a request for its device attributes (`CSI c`, which every terminal answers) sent after the query says that the color's is in too. crossterm 0.29 reads that answer as an event of its own and does not hand it to the program (nor does it deliver it as keys), so it cannot be watched for while the input thread is blocked in `read`; the quit therefore wakes that thread the way a resize would (SIGWINCH), and the thread asks through `supports_keyboard_enhancement` (the kitty flags and the device attributes, each of which it consumes), then tells the quit. The request is sent at the quit, not at the start, so that a terminal that never answers it costs input nothing (crossterm would wait 2 s for it); the quit's wait is then 100 ms at most (the delay under which a quit still feels immediate, and a hundred round trips of a local terminal; a link slower than that still leaks), and a terminal that answers the request (every one does) but not OSC 11 costs about a round trip.
- **VIS-5** [P2][M5] Even after the color vision deficiency simulation of Machado et al. (2009) for protan, deutan and tritan (severity 1.0), the change background still differs from the normal background by |Lc| ≥ 15 — U
- **VIS-6** [P2][M1] `.md`, `.txt`, `COMMIT_EDITMSG` and files without an extension wrap at 72 columns. Other files wrap at the screen width, and continuation rows show no line number — U
  - **Decided** (P2): 72 columns of text, not counting the gutter; a narrower screen wraps at its own width. A row breaks after a space when it can (and between CJK characters); a word longer than a row is cut at the edge. `App::wide` (false by default) lets a host with its own room, the editor, wrap prose at the width of its pane instead; `sim --plain` never sets it (UI-9).
  - **Decided** (P2): no row is ever wider than the screen, even on a screen narrower than the gutter plus one wide char or `<U+202E>` token.
- **VIS-7** [P2][M5] Wrapping applies kinsoku: `、。，．）」』】〕〉》ー…‥・！？` never start a row, and `（「『【〔〈《` never end one. A full-width character counts as 2 columns (unicode-width) — U (property test)
  - **Decided** (P2): when a row cannot satisfy kinsoku (a row of nothing but `、`), it breaks at the edge anyway.
  - **Decided** (P2): an emoji presentation sequence (a char followed by U+FE0F) takes 2 columns and a ZWJ sequence counts as one cluster, as grapheme-clustering terminals (Ghostty, kitty, WezTerm) draw them.
  - **Decided** (P2): a blank is never widened by U+FE0F. A ZWJ joins only an emoji to an emoji: before the ZWJ a wide pictograph, or a narrow one with its U+FE0F; after it a wide pictograph, or a narrow one followed by U+FE0F. `a` + ZWJ + `b` are three plain chars. That never reads narrower than `unicode-width` does a string (checked on every string of up to 6 chars over a few emoji, CJK, blanks, U+FE0F and ZWJ). A U+FE0F that follows a `^[` or `<U+202E>` token, or another U+FE0F, is not sent, since the terminal would widen the token's last char.
  - **Decided** (P2): a row breaks at the latest place after a space or between two wide chars where the rule allows it, otherwise at the nearest place where it does (a closing mark at the edge takes the char before it along, and a run of them takes more), otherwise at the edge. Never inside a cluster (before a joined emoji or a variation selector). The first row of a line may start with a closing mark and the last may end with an opening bracket: nothing can help that. Text without these characters wraps as it did before.
- **VIS-8** [P2][M1] A tab is displayed as 4 columns — U
  - **Decided** (P2): tab stops are every 4 columns, so text aligned with tabs stays aligned.
- **VIS-9** [P2][M1] State is shown in words. The status line has the form `path  line:col  MODE  modified · 3 changes on disk · conflicts with the disk` — U (golden)
  - **Decided** (P2): MODE is NORMAL, INSERT, VISUAL or V-LINE, and col counts characters from 1. Parts that don't apply are left out, and a count of one is singular. Other states (read-only with its reason, `deleted on disk`, a running filter) are added the same way, in the order: modified, read-only, `CRLF` (the line ending is not LF), `BOM` (the file has one), deleted on disk, changes on disk, conflicts with the disk (SAFE-7), filter. A change on disk is a run of marked lines next to each other (a block of ten added lines is one change). A message (an error, `search wrapped`) replaces the status line until the next key. While typing after `:`, `/` or `?`, the status line is the command line. An unnamed buffer shows `[no name]`.
  - **Decided** (P2): the path is the least informative part of the line (git hands over an absolute path, and so does a prompt file under `$TMPDIR`), so a line that does not fit shortens it first, from the left: `…/` and as many of its last components as fit, the file name at least, measured in columns (a wide char counts 2, VIS-7). Only when even `…/{file name}` leaves the line too long is the line cut at the width, from its end, so the position and the mode go last. A path of one component is never shortened, and a message replaces the line as before.
- **VIS-10** [P2][M1] The cursor shape also shows the mode (Normal: block, Insert: bar; none of them blink). Checked through the DECSCUSR output — U
  - **Decided** (P5): Visual mode uses the block, and the command line uses the bar.
- **VIS-11** [P4][M1] No decoration: no icons, no box drawing, no `~` past the end of the file, no highlighting of the cursor line — U (golden)
  - **Decided** (P2): the cursor line's number is drawn in the primary gray and the other numbers in the secondary: a cue to where the cursor is, not a highlighted bar (`cursorlineopt=number` in the author's nvim, for the same reason).
- **VIS-12** [P2][M1] The eyes find their place again: `n N * # ]c [c Ctrl-d Ctrl-u`, `:N`, `+N` and `file:line` put the cursor line in the middle of the screen, and moving the cursor keeps at least 8 lines of context above and below it (half the text area on a short screen) — U
  - **Decided** (P2): centering applies near the end of the file too; rows past the last line stay blank.

  - **Decided** (P2): a line taller than the screen scrolls by rows, so the cursor stays visible inside it.
## Keys

The vi grammar the hands already know, departing from vi wherever vi is worse than modern editors (P5).

- **VI-1** [P5][M1] Motions: `h j k l w b e W B E 0 ^ $ gg G f F t T ; , % Ctrl-d Ctrl-u zz`. A count can be given — U
  - **Decided** (P4): `%`, `n`/`N` and text objects ignore counts; `%` matches `()[]{}` only. `n N * #` are motions, so `dn` works, but an operator followed by `/` is cancelled (no `d/re`).
  - **Decided** (P5): word motions split CJK text where the script changes (Han, Hiragana, Katakana, and other wide characters are classes of their own), as in vim. vim's `:help exclusive` rules apply: an exclusive motion that ends in column 1 ends at the end of the line before, and becomes linewise if it started at or before the first non-blank (so `dw` on an empty line deletes it).
  - **Decided** (P5): `cw` on an empty line, and a `dw` that starts on one, end at the start of the next line, not at its first word (vim's `fwd_word` stops there). vim's `:help d` rule that makes a multi-line delete linewise is not taken (P4).
- **VI-2** [P5][M1] Editing: `x r J p P u Ctrl-r . i a I A o O dd cc yy D C Y >> <<` — U
  - **Decided** (P5): `Y` yanks to the end of the line, consistent with `D` and `C` (Neovim's default; vi's `Y` duplicates `yy`). `U` redoes, like Ctrl-r (vi's line-undo `U` is dropped). `>>` and `<<` shift by the indent unit of VI-6.
  - **Decided** (P5): `.` replays the keys of the last Normal-mode change, a new count replacing the old one; Visual-mode operations are not repeated, and a replay stops at the first key that fails. A paste inside an insert session belongs to that session's undo unit. After undo the cursor goes back to where it was before the command. A `.` among the keys of a replay does nothing, so a replay can never run for ever.
  - **Decided** (P1): counts saturate instead of overflowing, motions stop at the ends of the text, and a command that would produce more than 16 MiB of text (a held-down count on `p` or `r`) is refused with a message: the largest file sim is specified for is 10 MB (PERF-5), and an allocation far beyond it could abort the editor and lose the buffer. `.` after `u` repeats the last change, not the undo. `J` adds no space before a `)`. The message is `too large: 16 MiB at most`; the limit is on what the command adds.
- **VI-3** [P5][M1] The operators `d c y > < !` combine with a motion or a text object (`iw aw iW aW i" a" i' a' i( a( i[ a[ i{ a{ i< a<`) — U
  - **Decided** (P5): `!{motion}` opens the command line with `:{range}!`, as in vi.
- **VI-4** [P5][M1] Search: `/ ? n N * #`. Regular expressions use the syntax of regex-lite — U
  - **Decided** (P2, P5): a search wraps around the end of the file with the message `search wrapped`. It is smartcase: a pattern without an uppercase letter ignores case, one with an uppercase letter doesn't. `*` and `#` search for the whole word under the cursor, case-sensitively. Matches are not highlighted, since color is only for change.
  - **Decided** (P4): `*` and `#` put `\b` only at ASCII word edges (regex-lite's `\b` is ASCII-only). Matches never overlap.
  - **Decided** (P4): smartcase looks at letters only, not at escapes such as `\W`, and folds ASCII letters only (regex-lite's `(?i)`).
- **VI-5** [P5][M1] Visual mode: `v V o`. It takes the same operators as VI-3 — U
  - **Decided** (P2): the selection is shown with an underline (SGR 4), because color is reserved for change and reverse video is the fallback for change (VIS-4). `:` in Visual mode starts the command line with `'<,'>`.
  - **Decided** (P4): Visual mode takes motions, text objects, `o`, the operators and `:`; other Normal-mode commands (`p`, `J`, `u`, `.`, `/`) do nothing there. A text object widens a selection longer than one char.
- **VI-6** [P5][M1] Insert mode: typing, BS, Enter (keeps the indent of the line above), Tab (inferred from the file: a tab if the first indented line uses a tab, that many spaces if it uses spaces, otherwise 4 spaces), Ctrl-w, Ctrl-u, Esc — U
  - **Decided** (P5): BS at the start of a line joins it to the line above.
  - **Decided** (P5): Ctrl-[ and Ctrl-c leave Insert mode like Esc, since the hands know both. Moving with the arrows inside one insert session keeps it one undo unit.
  - **Decided** (P2): an indent that nothing was typed after is removed again when Insert mode ends or the line is left, so `o<Esc>` leaves no trailing blanks.
  - **Decided** (P2): it is the indent of `o`, `O`, Enter and `cc` that counts. A char or Tab typed on the line makes it text; BS, arrows within the line and the like do not.
- **VI-7** [P5][M1] ex commands: `:w [file]` `:w!` `:q` `:q!` `:wq` `:x` `:cq` `:e {file}` `:e!` `:{N}` `:{range}s/re/rep/[g]` `:{range}!cmd` `:seen`. Ranges are `N` `N,M` `%` `.` `$` `'<,'>` — U
  - **Decided** (P5): `:q` and `:e {file}` are refused while the buffer is modified (`:q!` and `:e!` discard). In the replacement of `:s`, `&` and `\1`–`\9` refer to the match, as in vi. `:x` writes only when the buffer is modified.
  - **Decided** (P1): a file arrives as an event, so the buffer may be edited between `:e` and the arrival. The App records the buffer's version when the command runs, and if it differs when the file arrives (an edit that was undone again gives the same version), it refuses the load with `not opened: edited meanwhile` instead of discarding those edits.
  - **Decided** (P4, P5): ranges have no `+N` offsets; `N,M` with M < N is swapped, numbers clamp to the file, and a bare range goes to its last line. `:e` without a file reloads (refused while modified); `:wq!` and `:x!` force the write. `:s` takes only the `g` flag, and `:s//rep/` reuses the last search pattern.
  - **Decided** (P1, P2): a range that doesn't parse is an error, never ignored. `:w !cmd` is refused (filters are `:{range}!cmd`). `:wq {other}` and `:x {other}` write the other file and don't quit while the buffer's own file still differs, and say so.
  - **Decided** (P1): `:w` is refused while an `:e` is still reading, an `Opened` nobody asked for is ignored, and every `:e` keeps its own expected version, so two quick `:e!` can't discard the edits made between them. When one of them loads, the next answer is checked against the new buffer as it was loaded (versions start again at 0): it loads only if nothing was typed since.
- **VI-8** [P5][M1] Every yank with no register name also sends the text to the system clipboard through OSC 52; a delete or a change fills only the registers (VI-15) — U
  - Changed from "a yank prefixed with `"+`": copying out to another program is the common case, and a prefix to remember costs keys without adding safety (P5). Deletes stay out of the clipboard so that `x` and `dd` never clobber what you copied.
  - Changed (2026-10-18, under the owner's delegation): this item began "There is only one register, with no register names". VI-15 gives the names back, and the unnamed register is the one this item is about: what `p` puts with no name, and what a yank with no name sends out. A yank with a name of its own does not (VI-15); `"+` and `"*` are the names that ask for the clipboard.
  - **Decided** (P2): inside tmux (`$TMUX` set) the sequence is also sent wrapped in tmux's passthrough, because tmux's default `set-clipboard external` drops it.
  - **Decided** (P2): a delete or yank of nothing leaves the register and the clipboard alone; `p` with an empty register says so.
  - **Decided** (P2): the message is `the register is empty`, for a name too (`"zp`).
  - **Decided** (P2): a yank says what it took (`3 lines yanked`, `12 chars yanked`), since the clipboard gives no other sign (the author's nvim flashes the yanked text; the core has no color to spare, and the editor lights the text a yank took, UI-13).
- **VI-9** [P3][M1] The kitty keyboard protocol is enabled without waiting for a query. Esc is handled without delay. Bracketed paste is enabled, and one paste is one undo unit — U
  - **Decided** (P1): a paste is always inserted at the cursor as text and never run as keys, whatever the mode (Visual ends first). On the command line, line breaks in a paste become spaces.
  - **Decided** (P1): without the kitty protocol (in tmux, over ssh), Esc and the next key can arrive together and look like Alt+key. sim has no Alt bindings, so Alt+key is read as Esc, then the key. A paste also ends a pending count or operator.
  - **Decided** (P5): a paste is text, not keys, so `.` does not repeat it: the change that `.` repeats is the keys that were typed.
- **VI-10** [P2,P5][M4] In Normal mode, full-width ASCII (U+FF01–FF5E) is read as half-width (so you can keep operating with the IME on) — U
  - **Decided** (P5): this applies to command keys in Normal and Visual mode and after an operator, but not to the character after `f F t T r`, nor in Insert mode or on the command line.
  - **Decided** (P5): the range is exactly U+FF01–FF5E, each read as the ASCII char 0xFEE0 below it. The ideographic space (U+3000) and the half-width forms next to the range are not read as anything.
- **VI-11** [P2][M4] If the environment variable `SIM_ON_NORMAL` is set (else `SI_ON_NORMAL`, its name before sim was one program), it runs asynchronously with `sh -c` every time Normal mode is entered (e.g. `fcitx5-remote -c` to turn the IME off) — I
  - **Decided** (P2): it also runs once at startup, since sim starts in Normal mode.
  - **Decided** (P2): an entry is a change of mode to Normal from Insert, Visual or the command line. Esc in Normal mode and the motions are not entries, and nothing runs on the way out. An empty one counts as unset; `SIM_ON_NORMAL` wins when both are set. `sim --plain` never runs it (PLAIN-1).
  - **Decided** (P1, P3): the shell runs it with `sh -c` in its own process group, with no input, reaps it from a thread, and drops its output and its failure: a hook can never block a key or write on the screen.
- **VI-12** [P5][M1] The keys every editor has work everywhere: the arrows, Home, End, PageUp, PageDown and Delete in Normal, Visual and Insert mode, and Left, Right, Home, End, BS and Delete on the command line — U
  - **Decided** (P5): Ctrl-h is Backspace, as in vi: a terminal set to send byte 8 for its Backspace key (PuTTY's option, `stty erase ^H`) has no other, and crossterm reads that byte as Ctrl-h.
  - **Decided** (P2): a command line longer than the screen scrolls to keep its cursor visible.
  - **Decided** (P2): it keeps the scroll it has while the cursor stays on the screen, and always the char before the cursor, so Home shows the start again.
- **VI-13** [P5][M1] Finishing takes few keys: Ctrl-s saves in every mode (Insert mode stays Insert mode); `ZZ` saves if modified and quits, like `:x`; `ZQ` quits without saving, like `:q!`; `q` quits like `:q` (as in less; sim has no macros). Ctrl-c with nothing to cancel says `q to quit` — U

- **VI-14** [P2][M4] Incremental search: while a `/` or `?` pattern is typed, the cursor jumps to the first match from where the search started (centered, VIS-12); Esc goes back there. Nothing is highlighted — U
  - From the author's nvim (`incsearch`, the default in vim 9 and Neovim): you see where you are going before Enter.
  - **Decided** (P2 over P3): on a 100,000-line file a key of the search line may take up to PERF-4's 50 ms, since it is a search (the line itself, `n`, and Enter cost the same). No other key is allowed this. The status line is the command line, so the search is the one thing on the screen that moves.
  - **Decided** (P1, P2): the cursor itself does not move while the line is typed: the screen is drawn around the first match as if it were there (centered, VIS-12; the number of that line is the cursor line's), and Enter then moves the cursor by searching as it always does, so the two cannot differ. Esc, and the Backspace that removes the `/`, have nothing to give back, a change that arrives from the disk meanwhile cannot leave the cursor or the view anywhere but where the search started, and the marks of DISK-6 stay on the lines the preview passes (a mark goes when the cursor enters its line; one that is shown for a moment and then gone unseen would defeat it). Each key searches again from the cursor; a pattern that is empty, does not compile, or matches nothing shows the cursor's own place, and the last search (`n`) is not touched.
- **VI-15** [P5][M1] Registers: `"a` to `"z` hold text of their own and `"A` to `"Z` add to it, `""` is the unnamed one (what `p` puts, and what a yank puts first, VI-8), `"0` the last yank, `"1` to `"9` the last deletes of a line or more, each new one pushing the others up a number, `"-` the small deletes, `"_` takes whatever it is given and holds nothing, and `"+` and `"*` write the system clipboard. The name goes before the command or between its count and the command (`"ayw`, `"Ayy`, `"ap`, `2"ayy`, `"a2yy`), in Visual mode too; `:registers` (`:reg`) says what they hold; `.` after `"1p` puts `"2p`, and then `"3p` — U, E
  - Changed from VI-8's "there is only one register" (decided 2026-10-18 under the owner's delegation, not by the owner). The premises are the owner's: on 2026-10-16 the owner proposed 「セキュアなRust製Vim」, and on 2026-10-17 settled sim's concept as 「隠れたものまで見える、セキュアな Rust 製 Vim」, to be called the next-generation Vim (新時代の Vim); and on 2026-10-15 the owner asked for 「viを変に意識しないでエディタとしてベストなキーバインド」. P5 has two halves, and the two sentences are one each: keep the vi grammar the hands already know, and depart from vi wherever it is worse. A Vim's `"a` is the first half, so a single register was a departure with nothing better to show for it; the second half still holds for what is left out (below). Macros (`q`) and marks (`m`) are not in this item: `q` still quits (VI-13).
  - **Decided** (P5): which registers there are is vim's, and the rules are vim's, checked against Neovim (`core/tests/oracle.rs`, `SIM_ORACLE_MODE=reg`, which runs three to five commands with names and counts and compares the text and what every register holds, its type and its text). A yank without a name fills `"0` (and is what the unnamed register points to); a delete or change fills `"1` when it takes a line or more or moves by `%`, `n`, `N`, `*` or `#`, and `"-` otherwise; with a name it fills that register, and `"1` as well when it takes a line or more. A name in capitals adds to the register: lines added to characters, or characters to lines, make the register whole lines, as in vim. `"_` leaves everything as it was, the clipboard too. Reading a register that holds nothing says `the register is empty` and does not move the cursor.
  - **Decided** (P2): what the oracle found (2026-10-18, Neovim 0.12.5, seeds 8 and 9, 6,000 cases each of three to five commands: yanks, deletes, changes and puts, with and without names and counts, and `.` after `"1p`; the cases in which nvim stops at a failing command, 832 and 783, are not compared). 4,829 were compared: the text and every register's type and text agree in all but 4, and the 4 are the editing's, not the registers': in each a `d` of a word or of a text object, with a count or repeated by `.`, at the end of a line or on an empty line, reaches another range in sim than in nvim (the linewise exception below among them), and the registers differ only because the text does. Two classes are left out on purpose (2,426 and 2,346 of the 6,000): a command that takes nothing (VI-8's Decided rule: the registers stay as they were, where vim's empty yank empties its register and points the unnamed one to it), and a `d{motion}` that takes a line break (vim makes it linewise when only blanks come before it and nothing after; sim keeps it charwise, so the register's type and the text follow, NOTES); a count on a text object, which VI-1 decided to ignore, is left out as in the other modes (382 and 402). Runs with a wider set of commands before these found the same kinds of difference (a text object `i(`, a count on `%` or `*`, the cursor after a yank of lines up or to the end of the file) and none in the registers' own rules: which register a delete fills, the shift to `"9`, the unnamed pointer, the append of capitals and its mix of lines and characters, `"_`, `"-` and `.`.
  - **Decided** (P1): a name that is no register fails with `not a register: x`, and nothing is done (the text is not touched, a count is dropped, Visual mode ends). The read-only registers of vim (`".`, `":`, `"%`, `"#`, `"/`, `"=`) are not here, for the same message: what they hold is not the user's text, and `"=` evaluates an expression.
  - **Decided** (P1, P2): memory only. A register is never written to the disk and is not in a snapshot (SAFE-6), and the registers are gone when sim ends. vim's `viminfo` (shada in Neovim) keeps them in plaintext in the home directory, so what was cut an hour ago (a password pasted for a moment, a token from the line that was deleted) stays on the disk for good, outside every promise P1 makes; a register that must outlive sim is a file, and that is what the user writes. The registers are shared by every file open in sim (the next file to be typed in is given them), so a yank in one file can be put in another, and they outlive the file they were made in.
  - **Decided** (P1, P2): the clipboard has the names `+` and `*`, for writing only. `"+y` and `"*y` do what a plain yank does, so `p` puts it; `"+d`, `"+c` and `"*d` also send what they took to the clipboard (a cut, the one deliberate exception to VI-8's rule that a delete stays out of it). A yank with any other name does not go out: it stays in sim, even over ssh and in tmux, where the clipboard is the terminal's and not the machine's, which is what `"ay` is for: text that must not leave. `"+p` and `"*p` do not read the clipboard: a terminal does not hand it over (OSC 52 has a query for it, and a terminal that answered it would hand the clipboard to any program that prints to the screen), and a program that reads it (`xclip`, `pbpaste`) is a program that plain mode (PLAIN-1) does not run. They say `the clipboard is write-only: paste with the terminal (bracketed paste)` and put nothing; the terminal's own paste is the way in, and it is one undo step that is never run as keys (VI-9).
  - **Decided** (P1): what a register holds is shown as the buffer shows it. `:registers` shows a line break as `⏎` and cuts each register to its share of the line (`…`: at most 16 characters, and at least 4, so that the last one is on the screen when there are not many), the unnamed first, those that hold nothing left out (`no registers` when none holds anything), on one line, as every message; a control character in them is drawn as `^[`, and a bidirectional control or U+200B as `<U+202E>` (SAFE-8), never raw. What is put from a register is inserted as text, so it is as visible as any other (SAFE-8).
  - **Decided** (P2): a yank into a name says what it took (`3 lines yanked`) and is lit for a moment (UI-13) as a plain one is, since the clipboard is not what tells the user it happened; `"_` yanks nothing and says nothing.
  - **Decided** (P4): a register is a rope, not a string: it shares its text with the buffer and with the undo steps, so `"1` to `"9` holding nine deletes of a 10 MB file cost nothing and a yank of the whole file into each of the 26 letters adds 0.1 MB. Measured on a release build (AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a busy machine with a load average of about 8; a file of 100,000 lines and 9.7 MB, 11.0 MB of heap when open; nine times `ggdG` and `u`): registers of strings added 87.4 MB (a peak of 113.8 MB) and `ggdG` took 1.7 ms; registers of ropes added 0.0 MB (a peak of 11.1 MB) and it took 0.18 ms; `"ay` of the whole file into each of `a` to `z`: 399.8 MB against 0.1 MB.
