  - **Decided** (P1): a host in the text that has letters that are not ASCII is an address, and is judged (the review: `[gооgle.com](https://evil.com)` with a Cyrillic `о`, and `[ｇｏｏｇｌｅ．ｃｏｍ](…)`, were no address at all, so no link was judged, and a reader sees google.com in both while a browser maps the fullwidth form to it). The fullwidth forms of ASCII (U+FF01 to U+FF5E) and the full stops of other scripts (`。`, `｡`, `․`, `﹒`) are mapped before the words are split, in every form of the text, and the result is judged as ASCII. A dotted word with a letter that is not ASCII in some label names a host when its last label is a top-level domain or a country code after the mapping and lower casing, or becomes one when the Cyrillic and Greek letters that look like an ASCII one are read as that one (`google.cоm`). It is honest when the target's host is the same string as written (the same chars after the same mapping and lower casing: `[例え.jp](https://例え.jp)`), or when each label that is not ASCII is the RFC 3492 punycode of it, lower cased, with `xn--`, and the others the same (`[例え.jp](https://xn--r8jz45g.jp)`, `bücher` is `xn--bcher-kva`; the encoder is `lie::punycode`); any other target lies, the real ASCII host of a look-alike too (`[gооgle.com](https://google.com)`), and an ASCII text against a target whose host is as written with such letters is odd, as it was. A homograph in the domain itself, where the text and the target are the same string (`[gооgle.com](https://gооgle.com)`), is out of MD-10's scope: nothing in the file says which of the two is the one the reader means. Residual: the last label of an internationalized top-level domain (`例え.みんな`) and a gTLD that is not in the list are not read, a letter that no table here knows is a look-alike, the compatibility mappings of Unicode beyond the fullwidth forms and the full stops (ligatures, superscripts, a katakana middle dot, a combining accent as a letter of its own: `u\u0308ber.de` is not `über.de`) are not made, and the labels are not normalized, so a text in the form that differs from the one of the target lies. Tests: `md_10_a_host_that_has_letters_that_are_not_ascii_is_an_address_and_is_judged`.
  - **Decided** (P2): what a word is an address by: a name with a dot is not one by itself (`Node.js`, `main.rs`, `README.md`, `Makefile.am`): a host needs a top-level domain from a list (`com org net edu gov mil int io dev app ai co jp uk de fr info xyz me us eu ru cn in br ca au nl se ch example`, and `biz name pro mobi asia tel travel jobs page cloud online site store tech shop blog live news link click top vip icu club space website world today one fun life email works wiki onion local internal lan corp home systems services solutions network digital agency design studio media finance money bank pay security support host download software games chat bot`), `www.`, an IP address, or a country code (the 249 of ISO 3166-1: `selection.on` and `x.zz` are no hosts) as its last label except the ones that end a source file (known limit (1) below); a path has a leading `/`, `./`, `../` or `~/`, or a `/` and a file with a name and an extension that has a letter (not `HTTP/1.1`, not `owner/.github`). A path in the text is judged only against a target that has no host (relative, or a file of the machine; known limit (6)), and one whose first segment is a host is that host (`/good.com/login`, also `//good.com/x`): a word is read as the reader reads it. A text whose target has no host and whose path ends with the word is honest whatever its last label (`[example.kr](docs/example.kr)`, `[config.h.in](build/config.h.in)`); where the target has a host the word is judged by the host, since `[paypal.com](https://evil.com/paypal.com)` would otherwise pass (so `[Makefile.in](https://github.com/x/blob/main/Makefile.in)` is marked: `in` is India's, and a rare file name is cheaper than a missed host). The test table: lies `paypal.com.mx`, `example.kr`, `shop.co.uk`, `good.de/login`, `www.good.zz`, `good.page`, `good.onion` against `https://evil.example`; honest `main.rs` → `src/main.rs`, `README.md` → `…/README.md`, `Makefile.am` → `…/Makefile.am`, `Node.js` → `https://nodejs.org`, `setup.sh`, `script.py`, `main.cc`, `a.so`, `parse.pl`, `lib.ml`. UI-1 gives magenta to what is hidden or dangerous, so the mark has to stay rare for it to mean something; a missed look-alike is a cost, a link in every README that cried wolf is one too.
  - **Decided** (P2): reference links are judged too (otherwise `[good.com][1]` with `[1]: https://evil.com` at the bottom walks around the mark), in the three forms, resolved by the definitions of the document, with labels matched as CommonMark matches them (case folded, runs of blanks one) and every definition of a label counted (the first wins in CommonMark, and sim does not know which a reader's renderer takes). The definitions are found after the markers of block quotes and list items (`> - > [1]: …`), whatever their indent, with the destination on the next line (after the same markers); a definition in a fenced block is taken for one (sim errs to the side of marking). An image's, a footnote's (`[^1]`), a task's (`[x]`), an inline link's and the label part's brackets are not uses, and neither is a pair that has an inline link in its text (`[[`0d8a6f1b19`](https://…)]`: a reference cannot have a link in its text). A use that a definition resolves is judged by it. A use whose text names a host (not only a path: `[ -d /tmp ]` is code far more often than a link) and that no definition resolves is marked when a definition may have been missed: the document was not looked through whole (past 2,000 lines or 120,000 chars, a line past 10,000 chars with a `]` in it, the budget of the scan), a line has a `]:` that was not read as a definition (a footnote's `[^1]:` is none), or a label has a letter with a case that is not ASCII (`ß` is `SS` to CommonMark and not to `to_lowercase`; a use with such a label is marked, and so is an ASCII use in a document that has such a definition; Japanese, which has no case, is read as it is). Otherwise it is no link in any renderer, and is not marked (a version of this rule that marked every such use made a JSON array in code, `["", "/index"]`, and `clusters[pick.id]` marks). The cue is where each part is drawn: on the line that has the use its text with its brackets (`[good.com]`, for the three forms, and not the label part `[1]`; over lines, the part of it on each line), and the target on the definition's line when a use of the part that was looked through makes it lie: the text is where the eye is, it is the part that says the address, and a block on a few chars is the least that cannot be missed. A definition is not marked for a use that was not looked at (a CHANGELOG of 7,000 lines has hundreds, and the first version marked them all): the use is marked where it is drawn.
  - **Decided** (P1): the mark of a link that lies is a security cue, like the hidden char's block (UI-15), so calm draws it too: the Painter's inverse of a hidden char, sealed, over the URL's and the link's looks. The flash of UI-19 and the frame of GIT-5 stay out of calm, which draws no interface layer; this is a char the reader must not miss and is not interface. In the window the target is the alert's pill (GUI-9); the scene golden `lie_dark` pins it.
  - **Decided** (P1): the links are found by the chars (a `](`, the pairs of brackets as CommonMark pairs them, raw HTML's `<a`), and not by the highlighter's link and URL spans: bat's Markdown takes a URL inside the strong text of a link's text for a bare URL and swallows the `](`, and a mark that a construct of the highlighter's could switch off would be one an attacker chooses to switch off. A file is Markdown for this when the highlighter's own table says so by its name (`syn::is_markdown`: bat's Markdown claims `md`, `mdown`, `markdown` and `markdn`, and no other name; the names of other tables are not sim's to list), and any line the highlighter gives a link or a URL is looked at in any file, so neither gate narrows the other; the references and the raw HTML of the document are for a Markdown file. The mark is there on the first frame, before the highlighter has answered, and no span says what is code (above). Tests: `md_10_every_name_that_the_highlighter_calls_markdown_has_the_marks_and_no_other_does`, `md_10_a_file_that_is_no_markdown_by_its_name_has_the_marks_where_the_highlighter_finds_links`.
  - **Decided** (P1): known limits, none of them closed because each costs more marks than it finds. (1) A name that ends in two letters that end a source file more often than they are a country (`rs md py sh pl ps cc so mk mm am ac ml pm tf gd kt sv hs ts js cs rb go el ex jl vb`) is not a host, so `[docs.rs](https://evil.example)`, `[lib.rs](…)` and `[bun.sh](…)` are not marked while `[main.rs](src/main.rs)`, `[README.md](…)` and `[Node.js](https://nodejs.org)` are honest. Closed cheaply it would not be: the rule that an honest target's host contains the text's first label admits `bunsh.com` for `bun.sh` and marks `[README.md](https://example.com/docs)`, `[build.rs](https://doc.rust-lang.org/cargo/…)` and `[install.sh](https://get.example.com/install)`, which are common and honest. (2) A top-level domain that is also a file extension (`zip`, `mov`) is not a host (`[invoice.zip](https://…)` is a download). (3) A homograph that is the same string in the text and in the target is not detected (the rule above): the mark is for a text that is not its target, and the read view does not hide the target for the reader to compare. (4) What a renderer builds from something that is not the file: a style that hides a part of the text, an image's pixels, a font. (5) Of the thousand top-level domains the list above has about ninety, and any two letters. (6) Closed 2026-10-18, under the owner's delegation: a path in a text is judged only against a target that has no host (relative, or a local file); against a target that has a host it is not. It was a known limit: `[/r/rust](https://www.reddit.com/r/rust)`, `` [`/dev/urandom`][14] `` and `` [`.cargo/config.toml`] `` were marked, 46 of the 128 marks on 6,000 real files and all of them honest, and a mark that is mostly wrong is a mark that the reader learns to pass (habituation to security warnings: Anderson et al., CHI 2015; Akhawe and Felt, USENIX Security 2013). What it costs is little: the browser shows the host, which a path does not name, and the suffix rule already let `./install.sh` go to `https://evil.example/install.sh`. A path whose first segment is a host is read as that host (`lie::address`), so `[/good.com/login](https://evil.com/login)` is still marked, and a path against a relative target that differs, a `file:` that differs, or a target that runs nothing (`tel:`, `javascript:`) still is. (7) A reference whose definition only a renderer's own extension finds (Pandoc's implicit heading references): CommonMark and GFM define a reference by `[label]: destination` and nothing else. (8) An address with a char fused to it that is part of no host, other than an `&` (`good.com:#443`, `good.com:-443`): the word is not an address.
  - **Decided** (P4): the document's scan and the pathological shapes, measured (release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a machine at load average 9 to 10). Looking through a document costs 400 µs for 2,000 lines of about 60 chars (a definition and a use of an address in every tenth line, median of 30; 69 µs for 300 lines), once for a version of the text, and only where a visible line has a definition or a use whose text names an address (`md_10_bench_the_scan_for_reference_links_takes_well_under_a_key`). The bench of the shapes (`md_10_bench_a_line_of_a_megabyte_and_a_document_of_long_lines`: 27 shapes, each as a line of a megabyte, as a screen of ten of them, as 2,000 lines of 10,000 chars looked through, and as the 60 lines of a frame of that document): the worst is 9.3 ms for a line of a megabyte (a host of 990 distinct letters that are not ASCII: its punycode is quadratic in the label, which a text of 1,000 chars bounds, and the frame's budget pays for), 25.8 ms for ten of them, 1.5 ms for the scan of 2,000 lines of 10,000 chars and 15.8 ms for a frame of 60 lines of 10,000 chars (a text of 400 tags, 240 times), the medians of the 27 shapes 0.10 ms, 6.3 ms, 0.30 ms and 1.5 ms. Looking through the same document with the code before this round (e8af3bb), in the same minutes, took 304 to 324 µs for 2,000 lines and 50 µs for 300.
  - **Decided** (P4): the real files, measured once with the detector as it stands (6,000 Markdown files of this machine: the repositories under `~/ghq`, the crates of cargo's registry, mise's installs, `/usr/share/doc` and the global node modules; 6,000 of them found by `find`, those of more than 6,000 lines skipped). 128 marks in 64 files. What they are: 21 are in sim's own README, CHANGELOG and SPEC, which are about this very feature; about 46 were a path in the text against a target with a host (known limit (6), closed since); 16 are the one security notice of Microsoft's packages that links the URL of the Security Response Center to an aka.ms short link, and a few are a text that names a host that its target is not, as they should be (`irc.mozilla.org` linked to `wiki.mozilla.org`, a googleapis.com URL linked to cloud.google.com); the rest are single links (`d3.link` linked to an anchor, a brace in an array of a document that has a line that may be a definition). The first run of the same files before this round's rules had marks on every badge and on every raw-HTML link around an image, on the hundreds of definitions of every long CHANGELOG and on the arrays of code, and on 3,638 lines in all. The highlighter hangs on 6 of the files (a fenced `js` block with a `return` and a JSDoc comment, five lines: `"```js\nfunction f() {\n  return start\n\n  /** @type {{State}} */\n}\n```"` makes `syn.spans` loop for good) — a pre-existing fault of the syntax set, not of MD-10, reported to the one who asked; closed since by the guard in syntect's parser (SYN-3: that document is a test of `syn_hang.rs`, parsed in a blink).
- **MD-11** [P2][S10] A quote whose first line is a callout's marker, `> [!NOTE]`, `[!TIP]`, `[!IMPORTANT]`, `[!WARNING]` or `[!CAUTION]` (GitHub's alerts; in any case), is a callout, and its kind is told by its word, always (so never by color alone, COLOR-3), and by GitHub's own icon where `zi` has the icons on (ICON-1); no look gives a kind a hue. In the read view the marker is drawn as the word, `Note`, `Tip`, `Important`, `Warning` or `Caution`, in bold in the text's own gray (with the icon and a blank before it when the icons are on); in flat and depth the bar of every line of the quote is the primary gray, where a plain quote's is the secondary, and in calm the bar is the quote's own `>`. The line the cursor is on, the selected lines, and every line while the read view is off show the file's own text, `[!NOTE]`, in bold. Any other `[!KIND]` is a plain quote — U (the finder and the plan), I, E
  - **Decided** (P1): which quotes, measured against GitHub's own renderer (`POST https://api.github.com/markdown` in mode `gfm`, 2026-10-18; its docs give the syntax and the five kinds and say nothing of case or placement). The marker is in the first line of the quote (the lines that start with `>` in the first column), as `>`, up to four blanks (one is the usual) and `[!KIND]`, in any case, and nothing but blanks after it; the quote has a later line with text on it; the highlighter calls its bar a quote's (so a line of a fence, which the highlighter calls `Band`, is none); the kind is one of the five. 24 forms are in a test (`md_11_a_quote_is_a_callout_where_githubs_renderer_makes_an_alert_of_it`), each with what GitHub made of it: the marker alone with no body, with a title after it (Obsidian's `[!NOTE]- title`) or with text after it, `[! NOTE]`, `[!!NOTE]`, `[!]`, `[!NOTE]:`, in strong or code, a quote in a quote, after a line of the quote, and in a fence stay quotes; any case, a tab or no blank after the bar, trailing blanks, a lazy line, blank quote lines before the body and a body that is a list or a fence make an alert. Where sim does not follow: GitHub makes an alert of a quote indented by one to three blanks and sim leaves it a quote (inside a list item GitHub leaves it a quote, and a line cannot tell that from the indent alone: sim takes the side that claims nothing); GitHub reads `\[!NOTE]` as the marker; and a quote whose first line is out of the spans the highlighter has given (more than a screenful above) is a quote until they come.
  - **Decided** (P1): MD-2 holds. What the read view leaves out of a marker passes through `may_hide(_, Why::Callout)`, which takes `[`, `!`, `]` and ASCII letters and nothing else, and only in a marker that is exactly one of the five names (so a SAFE-8 char in it makes it no marker at all, and is drawn as itself, UI-15); the word of the kind is put in their place one char for each, and is never longer than what it replaces (`Note` is four and `[!NOTE]` seven; with the icon and its blank, `Tip` is five and `[!TIP]` six). The click on the word is the next char that stays (MD-7).
  - **Decided** (P3): no hue, in any look. Decided on 2026-10-18 under the owner's delegation, not by the owner: the first version gave each kind a hue as text (NOTE the accent's blue, TIP the change's green, IMPORTANT the heading's cyan, WARNING the change's amber and CAUTION the change's red; GitHub's purple for IMPORTANT was left out, since magenta is the alert's alone), and the owner has twice answered the same question the other way. On 2026-10-17 a keyword of a TODO comment was refused a hue of its own, since five hues would be five more meanings for the hues that already mean changes, the accent and the alert (UI-7, the badge is the card's neutral surface with primary bold text), and the same day the owner ruled that the hue of a change must be unique among plain distractors, and that structure is weight and the grays, never a hue (COLOR-1). A callout's kind is the same kind of question: the accent's blue for a note would add a meaning to "here, where you act", and a change's green, amber and red would take away what they were kept for. So the word is the text's primary gray in bold, with the icon where `zi` has the icons on, in every look; and the bar of a callout in flat and depth is the primary gray, a plain quote's (and a bullet's, UI-8) staying the secondary: the two are told apart by form (the word and the icon on the first line, which a quote has none of) and by rank, lightness, which is what the owner's 2026-10-17 ruling gives to how much a thing is (the primary is |Lc| 90 against the page and the secondary 60; Mackinlay 1986, an ordinal channel for an ordinal fact). It is a loss against GitHub and render-markdown.nvim, which tell the five kinds apart at a glance by hue (the table below says so), and it is a change in two lines to give them back: a `Tone::Callout(kind)` with the five colors of the first version (they are in the history of this file) in place of `Tone::Call`, and the paint of the word. The test of the interface's budget (`color_1_...`) has callouts on its screen and no place for a hue on them.
  - **Decided** (P3): calm and `NO_COLOR` are as every look is now, and need no more than the others: the word is bold in the text's own gray, the icon too with `zi`, and the bar is the quote's own `>` (calm has no interface layer, and the change is its only chromatic background). Without color (COLOR-2) the word is bold and that is all.
  - **Decided** (P2): the icon is GitHub's own, from the octicons that a Nerd Font has (checked in the owner's UDEV Gothic 35NF): info `U+F449` for a note, light-bulb `U+F400` for a tip, report `U+F50A` for important, alert `U+F421` for a warning and stop `U+F46E` for a caution. With the icons off no private-use code point is drawn (ICON-1), and the word is there either way.
  - **Decided** (P2): against GitHub and render-markdown.nvim (GitHub's column is what its API made of the same text; render-markdown.nvim's is from its documentation, `doc/render-markdown.txt`, and was not run here):
    | | GitHub | render-markdown.nvim | sim |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | kinds | five | the five and about twenty of Obsidian's, as config | five; others stay quotes (loses to nvim) |
    | case of the marker | any | any (matched without case) | any |
    | the word | `Note`, from the kind | `Note`, set in config | `Note`, from the kind; the marker's letters are never shown |
    | icon | an octicon, always | a Material Design icon, as config | GitHub's octicon with `zi` only (loses to GitHub: no icon without a Nerd Font); the word is always there |
    | hue | fixed: blue, green, purple, amber, red | the colorscheme's Info, Success, Hint, Warn, Error | none: the word (always) and, with `zi`, GitHub's octicon tell the kind (loses to GitHub and nvim: COLOR-1, the owner's 2026-10-17 rulings) |
    | the bar | a left border in the hue | `▋` in the hue | `▌` in the primary gray (flat, depth), a plain quote's in the secondary; calm `>` |
    | background | none | none in its docs | none; calm none by rule |
    | without color | the word and the icon (its HTML has both) | its docs say nothing | the word, bold; no color is written (COLOR-2) |
    | the raw text | the file view's source | the cursor's line is shown as typed | the cursor's line, the selected lines and the whole file with the read view off, as typed; the marker in bold and the hue there too |
    | an indented, nested or listed quote | alert at 1 to 3 blanks outside a list, else a quote | not documented | a quote (loses to GitHub for 1 to 3 blanks) |
    | a hostile char in the marker | not examined | not examined | no marker; the char is drawn as itself (UI-15), tested |
    | settings | none | many | none (CONCEPT: no config files); `zi` and `<Space>um` |

## The tutor

- **TUTOR-1** [P2][S10] `:tutor` opens the lessons in a scratch buffer: about fifteen minutes of short lessons, in the language of the locale (TUTOR-3), with exercises that are graded as the text changes (TUTOR-2). Nine lessons in the editor, eight with `--plain`, which has no panel, picker, looks or leader: moving around (`h j k l`, `w b`, `0 $`, `gg G`, counts), typing text (`i a I A o O`, Esc), deleting and changing (`x dw dd cw r`, `d` and `c` with any move), undo (`u`, Ctrl-r), copy and paste (`yy p P yw`; what is deleted is copied), searching (`/ n N *`, `:s`, `:noh`), saving and quitting (`:w :q :wq :q!`, SAFE-7's copy before `:w!` or `:e!` gives a version up), what sim shows that other editors hide (SAFE-8's hidden chars, MD-10's links that lie) and, in the editor only, the finder (Ctrl-p), the search of contents (Ctrl-f), the tree (Ctrl-n), the looks (`zl`, `zi`) and `<Space>bk`. The buffer has no file and nothing typed in it writes one, or a snapshot of it: `:w`, `:w name`, `:w!`, `:wq`, `ZZ`, Ctrl-s and a tick or a signal write nothing; a write is answered as one that failed, with `the tutor is a scratch copy: nothing was written (:q! leaves)`; `:q!` closes it. `:tutor` again goes to the open one; `:e name` from it opens that file as ever, and when the buffer becomes a file the lesson is over — U (the text and the grading), I, E
  - **Decided** (P1): the tutor is a buffer of the core's App with no path (`App::new(None, ..)`), so the core's own rules hold for it without a line of the core's changing: `:w`, `:wq`, `:x` and `ZZ` have no file name to write, and `:w name` is a write of a file that does not exist. The core says `no file name` to the first, and the lesson says that the tutor is a scratch copy (it says so of `:w name`, and a lesson that says two things about writing is worse than one): sim takes the core's words at the place where an App's effects pass (`Sim::run`), puts the lesson's in their place and renders the frame again, and the core's status line, which names a buffer with no path `[no name]`, is `[tutor]` in the band (the tab already is). What sim adds is at the one place all of an App's effects pass (`Sim::run`): a `Write` is not passed on and is answered with `Event::Saved(Err(..))`, so that the App does not wait for it and says why (the buffer stays modified, nothing is quit); a `Snapshot`, `Unsnapshot`, `Remember` and `Keep` are dropped, and the ticks that ask every buffer for a snapshot skip it. A snapshot is a write too, into the private state directory (SAFE-6), and a lesson's text is not worth recovering after a crash, nor to be offered back as "recovered text" in the next run: "the tutor never writes on its own" holds for the state directory as it does for the working one. A test types every command and key that could write, with a file of the same name there, and finds the directory, the files and the state directory as they were, and no effect for the buffer but the clipboard's (a `dd` is copied, as ever).
  - **Decided** (P2): `:q` needs `!` because the exercises change the text, and the first lines of the lesson say so; `:tutor` is a command line's, so it needs a buffer: an editor started with no file and none opened cannot reach it (NOTES). In plain mode the tutor is one buffer more over the unnamed one, and `:q!` goes back to it. The lessons say to jump to one with `/^## 3` and Enter: the `^` is what makes it work, since the sentence that says how and the list of lessons would match a plain `## 3` first (a test searches for each of the nine in English, Japanese and Russian, the core's `regex-lite` taking the anchor).
  - **Decided** (P2): why the lessons are as they are. The sources are named with what was checked: the bibliographic details were checked on 2026-10-18 and what each says is from its abstract or a summary of it, not from the whole paper. (1) The immediate mark: Neovim's own `:Tutor` does it (its lesson says: "when there is a ✗ sign at the left, you will be required to modify text, and once the changes are done correctly, that sign changes to ✓", `runtime/tutor/en/vim-01-beginner.tutor`; the docs are neovim.io/doc/user/pi_tutor.html). Feedback on the task itself is one of the strongest influences on learning, and its form and timing matter (Hattie and Timperley 2007, "The Power of Feedback", Review of Educational Research 77(1): 81-112); applied studies, as against word-list experiments, have favored feedback at once (Kulik and Kulik 1988, "Timing of Feedback and Verbal Learning", Review of Educational Research 58(1): 79-97, known here through others' summaries). The claim is no larger than that: in a tutor for Lisp the timing of the feedback did not change the learning rate, the time taken or the post-test (Corbett and Anderson 1990, "The Effect of Feedback Control on Learning to Program with the Lisp Tutor", Cognitive Science Society), so the mark is at once because it costs the learner nothing and tells them they are right without waiting for an end, and not because it was shown to teach faster. (2) Short lessons: working memory holds about three to five chunks, and Miller's seven was a rough estimate (Cowan 2001, "The magical number 4 in short-term memory", Behavioral and Brain Sciences 24(1): 87-114), and a task that loads it with much at once teaches less (Sweller 1988, "Cognitive load during problem solving: Effects on learning", Cognitive Science 12(2): 257-285). No lesson introduces more than five groups of keys that go together (`h j k l` is one), and each has an exercise. (3) Recall and mixing: practice testing and distributed practice are the two techniques of ten that Dunlosky and others rated of highest utility, and interleaved practice moderate, its evidence limited (2013, "Improving Students' Learning With Effective Learning Techniques", Psychological Science in the Public Interest 14(1): 4-58); a test beats rereading after a week (Roediger and Karpicke 2006, "Test-Enhanced Learning", Psychological Science 17(3): 249-255), and problems of mixed kinds beat the same problems blocked by kind after a week (Rohrer and Taylor 2007, "The shuffling of mathematics problems improves learning", Instructional Science 35: 481-498). So a row names the keys its own lesson teaches (1.1 and 1.2 use `x` before lesson 3 teaches it, and say so); from lesson 4 on the rows ask for a result, and the keys of earlier lessons (`dw`, `dd`, `j`, `p`, `x`) have to be recalled to get it (4.1, 5.2, 6.1, 7.1 and 8.1 each need one, and name the lessons they come from). What is not known: the tutor was not tried on learners. What is tested is that each exercise can be done with the keys of its lesson, by a test that types them and sees the mark turn.
- **TUTOR-2** [P2][S10] A line that starts with `[ ] ` and an id (`1.1`) is an exercise's row, and is drawn with a mark in the space of the brackets, `[✗]` or `[✓]`, which is a check as soon as the lines under the row are the lines the exercise asks for (blanks at the end of a line do not count) and a cross again when they are not (an undo turns it back, a redo forward). The exercises of the last lesson ask for something sim shows rather than for text, and are checked once it has been seen: the finder (Ctrl-p) or the search of contents (Ctrl-f) open, the tree shown or hidden, the look switched, `<Space>bk` pressed; a mark of that kind stays once it is a check, and only what is done while the lesson is the file shown counts. When an exercise has just been done the status line says so (`✓ 1.1 done · 2 of 15`). The text of the buffer is not touched: the mark is the screen's, and is not in what is yanked, searched or written down — U (the grading), I, E
  - **Decided** (P2): the row is found by its id and the lines under it are compared, so the lines above can be deleted, added or moved without the grade of the others changing; a row that was typed over has no mark. An exercise gives its lines as they are (the work) and as they are to become (the solution) in the file, and the buffer has the first: the same exercises in every language by a test, and each solved by the keys of its lesson by a test.
  - **Decided** (P2): the marks are drawn over the frame's rows in `Sim::frame`, before the colors and the layout read it, at the char of the row where the text of the line has its `[ ]` (the digits of the number are counted, as for every row), and only if the chars there are `[`, a blank, a mark and `]`; a row that is cut or wrapped has the mark on its first row. No color: the shape says it (COLOR-1), `✓` and `✗` are one cell wide and the columns do not move.
- **TUTOR-3** [P2][S10] The language is that of `LC_ALL`, else `LC_MESSAGES`, else `LANG` (the first that is not empty; `C` and `POSIX` are a choice, and English), and is one of the README's ten: English, Japanese, Simplified and Traditional Chinese (`zh_TW`, `zh_HK`, `zh_MO` and a `Hant` script), Korean, Spanish, Portuguese (any `pt` is Brazil's), French, German and Russian. Any other, an unset locale and a code that is garbage are English. A line is at most 70 columns as the core counts them, so that the lessons fit an 80-column terminal with its number column — U, I
  - **Decided** (P2): `LANGUAGE` (gettext's list) is not read: it is a preference of a program that translates by message, and the tutor is one text. The eight languages other than English and Japanese were written from the English and are awaiting native review (NOTES).
- **TUTOR-4** [P4][S10] The lessons are text in the repository, `sim/tutor/<lang>.txt`, one file for each language, and the binary holds them packed: `sim/tutor/tutor.deflate`, made by `scripts/tutor-pack.sh` from the ten files, and a test fails when the packed file is not exactly their text. A file has the lines of the buffer and, for the exercises, `@ line` for a line the work is to become, `@! name` for an event to be seen, `\u{200B}` for a char the file must not hold itself, and `%editor` to `%end` for the lessons that plain mode does not have — U
  - **Decided** (P4): the size, measured (release build, 2026-10-18): the binary was 5,184,312 bytes before and is 5,224,080 now, under the limit of 5,242,880 with 18,800 to spare; the ten files are 58,742 bytes and the packed text 21,446, and the rest of the 39,768 is code. Compression was measured against the alternatives before it was chosen (on the first version of this code, and the code has grown by a few hundred bytes since): gzip read with `flate2`'s `GzDecoder` cost 32,880 bytes of decoder (5,255,656 in all, over the limit by 12,776), because it is a second instance of the code that inflates; raw deflate through `flate2::bufread::DeflateDecoder` on a slice is the same instance that syntect's syntaxes are read with, and cost about 300; the text uncompressed would have made about 5,261,400. `flate2` is a direct dependency of the editor for it, in the allowlist of `scripts/check.sh` with the reason in `sim/Cargo.toml`; it is in the tree already with the same features, no crate is added, and `Cargo.lock` gains one line.

## Color

- **COLOR-1** [P3][S6] One hue, one meaning, across all of sim. Neutral (the primary, secondary and faint grays, and bold) is all structure: text, line numbers, every icon, tree names at rest, markdown's markers, bullets and quotes, guides, whitespace marks, the colorcolumn, the scrollbar's handle, the status line's text. Change is green for what comes, amber for what changed and red for what goes, and appears nowhere in the chrome but the tree's names, letters and folder letters, the sign column and the scrollbar's marks, the counts `+n ~n -n`, the tab's changed-on-disk arrow and the tints of flat and depth. Accent, blue, means "here, where you act": the mode's block when it is not Normal and the position's after it, the shown tab's bar, a search's matches and their marks, a link and the yank's flash. Alert, magenta, is only for what is hidden or dangerous: an untrusted root, git not run for safety, a server with no sandbox, and a char that is not shown as itself (UI-15); nothing else in the text may be magenta, syntax included. Kind, cyan, is what the syntax gives functions, and tells what a thing is where the chrome has no hue for it: a heading (UI-8). Syntax keeps its hues inside the text. In calm the change keeps its one blue (P3), so calm's chrome has no accent: bold or inverse instead — U, I
  - **Decided** (P3): the owner's call, 2026-10-17, on seeing hues with no clear meaning (amber for modified, for the cursor's number and for a heading; green for added and for a heading; red for deleted and for a heading; a color for each type of file; a teal bar and teal bullets): the hue of a change must be unique among plain distractors for it to pop out (CONCEPT finding 8: Treisman & Gelade 1980; Duncan & Humphreys 1989), and every other use of a change's hue takes that away. Structure is therefore weight and the grays, never a hue.
  - **Decided** (P2): what that took out: the icons' colors by type and the folders' blue (icons are the secondary gray); the headings' colors by level (bold at every level, a first-level one on the band); the bullets' and the quote bar's hues (secondary); the cursor line's amber number (the primary gray, bold); the rename's blue (amber, with its `R`); the modified dot's amber on the tab and the status line (bold: unsaved is not a change of the file against HEAD); the diagnostics' red and amber on the tab (their shapes `✘ ▲` say it); the TODO keywords' five hues (one neutral badge, UI-7); the likely files' blue `•` (secondary); the modes' green, purple and amber (the accent for every mode but Normal, whose block is gray).
  - **Decided** (P2): the roles are in the code, not in each place that draws: `color::Role` is Neutral, Change, Accent and Alert, and the chrome asks `Tones` for a role's color (`change_fg(Chg::Add | Mod | Del)`, `accent_fg`, `accent_block`, `alert_fg`, `alert_block`, `gray`), never for a hue; `Tones::roles` lists every color the chrome has with its role. Five hues are derived from the terminal's palette (UI-1) and are at least 40 degrees apart; the neutral surfaces are mixes of the page toward secondary and have no hue but the page's own.
  - **Decided** (P2): the line numbers other than the cursor's are secondary, and in depth fade with their distance from the cursor by the text's own curve and their own floor, 40 (LOOK-6: the gutter has a paint of its own, `Paint::soft`); flat and calm are uniform. The cursor's line is the primary gray and bold.
  - **Decided** (P1): the alert coordinates with the core, which shows a char that must not reach the terminal as `^X` or `<U+XXXX>` in the text's own style (SAFE-8): that is not restyled. The alert is the status line's: the slot's `untrusted` and a server's alert, and the message that git was skipped (`git skipped: ...`, MARK-1), which is drawn in it for as long as it is on the row.
  - **Decided** (P2): the budget is a test (`color_1_...`): every color `Tones::roles` lists has a hue that belongs to its role (a neutral surface has no more hue than the page, a tint of a pale page may have none), no two of the five hues are closer than 40 degrees, no look of markdown has a hue (`paint::syntax_hue` is none for all of them and some for code's), and on screens of flat and depth (the text, the tree with every status, Insert and Visual mode, the tabs with a modified file, one changed on disk and a server's counts, an untrusted root) every cell that has a hue is where its role may be: a change's in the tree, the sign column, the ruler, the status line's counts, the tab's dot and the tints of changed rows; the accent in the status line only when the mode is not Normal, on the shown tab's bar and on a link; the alert only on the slot's word.
  - **Decided** (P2): the picker (PICK-1 to PICK-5) and the highlight of a search (UI-14) take only what the budget has: the window, the rule, the borders and the preview are neutral surfaces and grays (the preview's syntax colors are the text's own, SYN-1); the accent is the selected row (its block and the `▎`), the matched chars of the list, the matching line of the preview (its block, and on it the matched chars on the accent's solid color) and a search's matches in the text. The solid color is `Tones::accent_solid` (the accent's text color as a background, and the text on it), and `roles` lists it. The cell-by-cell test walks the screen with a picker open (a content search and the file finder, in flat and depth) and with a search lit, and every hue it finds is the accent's and inside the window or on a match.
  - **Decided** (P3): the owner's call, 2026-10-17, on seeing that a heading looked exactly like bold text (README's `**…**` and `## …` differed only by their `#`s): what a thing IS (a heading, code, a link) is told by hue; how much it is, or what rank it has (a heading's level, a task done or not, the distance of a row), by lightness and weight. Hue is a nominal channel and lightness an ordinal one (Mackinlay 1986), so each is used for the kind of fact it carries. Step D took Markdown's hues away to give a change one hue only; this takes back the one hue that nothing else in the chrome uses, cyan, for headings, and gives the others no new ones. Magenta stays the alert's alone: a target is found slower among distractors of its own color (Duncan & Humphreys 1989), so no look of the text is magenta (numbers and constants are the strings' green, SYN-1).
  - **Decided** (P2): `Role::Kind` is the sixth meaning, and `Tones` has its colors: the heading's text and the two bands (UI-8), all of the hue SYN-1 gives functions (the terminal's cyan when it answered, else the OKLCH hue 195), the text moved until |Lc| ≥ 75.5 against the stronger band, so it reads on both bands and on the page (UI-12). Six hues are at least 40 degrees apart (cyan is 50 from green and 55 from blue). The budget test (`color_1_...`) takes the cyan in: a heading's text and band are where it may be, no look of any kind is magenta (the colors of `Syntax::hue` on the eight backgrounds, and SGR 35 nowhere), and the hidden chars of UI-15 are the alert's only place in the text.

- **COLOR-2** [P2][S5] No color at all where it is not wanted or cannot be had: `NO_COLOR` set and not empty (no-color.org), or a terminal that cannot do 8 colors (`TERM` unset, `dumb` or `vt…`, or terminfo's `colors` below 8). sim then writes no color in any look (no SGR 30-38, 40-48, 90-97 or 100-107) and does not ask the terminal for its colors (OSC 11 and OSC 4). The interface is drawn all the same, and what colors said is said by weight, underline, slant and inverse video: the change style and every solid block (the mode, a badge, the current match, a yank's flash, a changed word) are inverse, a hidden char inverse and bold, the other matches of a search underlined, the open file's row in the tree bold, the secondary gray and comments faint; a tint or a surface says nothing and is dropped — U, I, E
  - **Decided** (P2): `io::mono(NO_COLOR, TERM, colors)` decides: any value of `NO_COLOR` but the empty one says no color (`0` too, as no-color.org has it), and it wins over `COLORTERM=truecolor`. terminfo's `colors` is read from the compiled file, not by running `tput` (PLAIN-1 would forbid a program): `$TERMINFO`, `~/.terminfo`, `$TERMINFO_DIRS`, then `/etc`, `/lib` and `/usr/share/terminfo`, in `dir/t/term` and (macOS) `dir/74/term`, in the 16-bit and the 32-bit format; a `TERM` that is a path is not looked up, and a file that cannot be read says nothing, so the name rules.
  - **Decided** (P2): `Syntax::mono` turns `paint::sgr` into `mono_sgr`: the style as the core draws it without a palette (faint, reverse), a look's weight, slant and underline, and the paint's `Cue` (`Solid` inverse, `Under` underlined, none dropped), which `layout` sets where a paint stands for more than a surface. `Sim::new` gives the interface a dark page to be laid out on (so flat and depth keep their boxes, tabs and bar) whose colors are never written, a reply to a color query is ignored, and `Tones::mono` lets `layout` choose another carrier where a surface was the only one. `sim --plain` is calm and not mono: it asks for the background, as the core always did.
  - **Decided** (P2): a start with no `TERM` is a start without color, and asks the terminal for nothing: not the background (VIS-4's OSC 11), not the ANSI colors (OSC 4), `+q` or not, and a quit waits for no answer (not even the 100 ms it allows a terminal that was asked and said nothing). A CI runner has no `TERM` (GitHub's Ubuntu and macOS runners, 2026-10-18), and four tests of VIS-4 failed there for want of a terminal that answers: they ran sim in a pty with the caller's environment. A test that runs the binary now gives it an environment of its own and nothing of the caller's terminal (`TERM`, `COLORTERM`, `TERM_PROGRAM`, `GHOSTTY_*`, `KITTY_*`, `WEZTERM_*`, `TMUX`; scripts/pty_bench.py and the e2e suites, which run the cases under `env -i`), and `pty_bench.py --no-term` is the start where `TERM` is unset (`color_2_a_start_with_no_term_asks_the_terminal_nothing_and_a_quit_waits_for_none`).
- **COLOR-3** [P2][S5] What a screen says is also said without its colors: in the sign column a changed line is `┆`, an added one `▎` and a deleted one `▁` (they were two bars that differed by hue); a folder shows the letter of the strongest git status below it (`M A U D !`), not a dot; a tab says "changed on disk" with `↯` and "unsaved" with a bold `●`; a changed word, a hidden char and the mode are told by inverse video, inverse and bold, and the word `NORMAL`, `INSERT`, `VISUAL`; the scrollbar's handle is a `║` in the column and not an inverse block (UI-11); the changes view says what a file is by its letter (`M A D R U`) and its numbers (`+n −n`), and the read view's table (MD-3) is drawn with `│` `├─┼─┤` where the interface is drawn and `|` and `-` where it is not, so that no row or column of it is told by a color or by a surface; none of this needs a Nerd Font (the icons are off then, ICON-1). A golden test draws the screens with no color (COLOR-2) and checks that these stay apart — U (golden)
  - **Decided** (P2): the check is on the screen as a terminal would show it: the bytes of the Painter, read back into a grid of chars and attributes (`.` plain, `b` bold, `f` faint, `i` italic, `u` underline, `r` inverse, `R` inverse and bold, `c` faint italic), in `tests/golden/color_3_*.txt`, and any color among the sequences fails the test.
- **ICON-1** [P2][S5] The icons need a Nerd Font, and a terminal does not say whether it has one. They start off on `TERM=linux`, `dumb` or `vt…` and in a locale that is not UTF-8 (`LC_ALL`, then `LC_CTYPE`, then `LANG`); elsewhere they start on only where the environment shows a terminal that brings Nerd Font symbols with it (Ghostty, kitty, WezTerm) and off, with the hint `zi: icons (needs a Nerd Font)` on the status line once, anywhere else (never in plain mode, PLAIN-1). What `zi` chose last (the file `icons`) always wins. With icons off no private-use code point (U+E000-F8FF, planes 15 and 16) is drawn anywhere: not in the tree, the tabs, the status line or its separators — U, I, E
  - **Decided** (P2): the terminals, read from their source: Ghostty embeds a symbols-only Nerd Font (`src/font/embedded.zig`) and sets `TERM_PROGRAM=ghostty` and `GHOSTTY_RESOURCES_DIR`; kitty has a builtin one (its FAQ: "kitty has a builtin NERD font and will use it for symbols not found in any other font") and sets `KITTY_PID`; WezTerm bundles one (its font docs: "WezTerm bundles JetBrains Mono, Nerd Font Symbols and Noto Color Emoji") and sets `TERM_PROGRAM=WezTerm` and `WEZTERM_PANE`. tmux sets `TERM_PROGRAM=tmux`, so the other variables are read too; they reach a session inside tmux, and not one over ssh, which gets the hint. Not verified here: iTerm2, Alacritty, Windows Terminal, and a terminal inside a font that has the symbols patched in (a user with one runs `zi` once).
  - **Decided** (P2): the hint is given once, ever: its marker is the file `icons-hint` in the state directory, written when the hint is due, and without a state directory there is nowhere to remember it and so no hint. A remembered `on` or `off` is never overridden, and a file with anything else in it is no choice.
  - **Decided** (P2): the hint is never given in plain mode, whether `--plain` was given or the world is privileged (PLAIN-1): `zi` is refused there, so the hint would name a key that does nothing, and it would use up the marker, so that the editor never gave it. `shell::begin` tells `io::start_icons` that the start is plain, and then the marker is neither read nor written; `Sim::new` shows no hint in plain mode whatever its `Env` says. What `zi` chose last is still read. The editor's first run after plain ones gives the hint once, as before.
  - **Decided** (P2): the scan is a test, not a promise: every frame of every Rig test (`tests/common`) and every golden is checked for private-use code points when the icons are off, and so is every screen an E2E `wait_for` sees (unless the case turns icons on).

## Speed (release build)

- **SPEED-1** [P4][S1] With the panel open and highlighting on, a key on a 100,000-line file takes at most 1 ms at p99 (the core's PERF-2 holds) — B
  - **Decided** (P4): measured on a release build (AMD Ryzen 9 9950X) with the panel open beside a 100,000-line file and some 300 files in the tree, the time of a key is `Sim::update` plus `Term::draw` into memory. At 80x24 the p99 is 70 us for motions, 56 us for insert and 59 us for delete; at 240x60 167 us, 133 us and 169 us; the keys of the panel itself take 14 us at most. `cargo test --release -p sim --test speed_1 -- --ignored bench --nocapture` prints them. With S3 (colored text, the panel's marks and a history of 1,000 files): at 80x24 the p99 is 90 µs for motions, 78 µs for insert and 84 µs for delete; at 240x60 410 µs, 215 µs and 210 µs; the keys of the panel itself take 114 µs at most. The spans are those of the window on screen, asked for from the highlighter's thread (SYN-3); the bench delivers them once and ignores the requests of the keys, since what it times is the main thread. Measured again on the core's M3, with the painter and the panel counting columns by the core's `width` (2026-10-16, a busy machine: load average about 3.5; the worst of three runs): at 80x24 the p99 is 186 µs for motions, 129 µs for insert and 139 µs for delete; at 240x60 591 µs, 390 µs and 381 µs; the keys of the panel itself 43 µs (80x24) and 135 µs (240x60). The longest key is a `%` on the 100,000-line file (2.4–3.4 ms, once in 4,000 keys). That is slower than the S3 numbers above (taken on another day, against the core's tree as it was then); the cause is not measured: the column rule of `layout::shown` is not it (the old, cheaper rule gives the same p99) and the machine was busy. Still under 1 ms at p99 (the largest p99 is 0.59 ms).
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again on the core's M5 with S4 (2026-10-16, a busy machine: load average 3.8, an Android emulator and a browser running; one run): the keys at 80x24 have a p99 of 207 µs for motions, 155 µs for insert, 160 µs for delete and 42 µs for the panel's own; at 240x60 596, 385, 366 and 130 µs. The mouse is a fifth kind of the bench (PTR-1): clicks in the text, drags (a press, four moves, a release), six notches of the wheel over the text and eight over the panel, at random places of the same file. At 80x24 the p99 of an event is 369 µs (a click 155 µs, a drag 211 µs, a wheel notch 477 µs, over the panel 110 µs); at 240x60 1.1 ms (click 863 µs, drag 1.1 ms, wheel 1.1 ms, over the panel 649 µs; the worst a wheel notch, 3.7 ms). The per-event cost is one or two renders of the App's frame, which cost what the data makes them (a line of 3,000 chars wraps into many rows at every render); an event of the mouse is bound at 2 ms, twice a key's, because a wheel notch and the first move of a drag render twice. Nothing is asked of the highlighter by a mouse event that a key would not ask.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with the looks (LOOK-1 to LOOK-9, 2026-10-16, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a busy machine: load average 2.4 when the run started and 10 when it ended; the other machine's jobs came and went, and a run of the whole bench takes four minutes). The bench runs each kind for calm and for depth (with a palette and truecolor, so that the tints, the gradients, the boxes and the card are drawn), and has a sixth kind, REVIEW (`]c` and `[c` among ten proposals that are all on the screen of the 100,000-line file; measured in a second run, with load around 7). The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 303 µs for motions, 237 µs for insert, 185 µs for delete, 74 µs for the panel's own keys and 506 µs in REVIEW; of a mouse event 536 µs (a wheel notch 772 µs). At 240x60: 967 µs for motions, 618 µs for insert, 551 µs for delete, 249 µs for the panel, 744 µs in REVIEW and 942 µs for a mouse event (a wheel notch 1.2 ms; the mouse has 2 ms). Calm on the same runs: 281, 160, 291, 68 and 455 µs at 80x24; 572, 669, 743, 202 and 450 µs at 240x60. A frame of depth costs about 100 µs more than calm's at 240x60 in `Sim::update` (`layout::body` builds a Line for every row of the screen) and about 100 µs in the Painter (fades and tints split the runs); the Painter keeps the SGR sequences it has made and `color::fade` its results, which took a REVIEW frame from 320 to 100 µs of painting. The bound is the machine's when it is busy: the same bench run when another job's load was over 8 failed in calm (REVIEW at 240x60, 1.0 ms) as well as in depth (motions at 240x60, 1.1 ms). `SPEED_1_ONLY=review cargo test --release -p sim --test speed_1 -- --ignored bench --nocapture` runs the kinds named, and each line printed has the mean time of `Sim::update` and of the Painter.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with the tree of the interface (UI-2, UI-3: icons, git letters, the header and a tab; `Env.chrome` and `Env.icons` on; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a busy machine: load average 9 at the start and 8 at the end; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel`). The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 244 µs for motions, 168 µs for insert and 87 µs for the panel's keys; at 240x60: 599 µs, 487 µs and 223 µs (calm on the same run: 223, 162 and 56 µs; 465, 410 and 149 µs). In this run depth costs more than calm by 32 µs in `Sim::update` and 65 µs in the Painter for a key of motions at 240x60 (means: update 334 against 302 µs, painter 93 against 28 µs). The icons are not cached: a name is lower-cased and looked up in a table of 41 entries for each of the panel's rows, which is within those 32 µs. The longest key is a `%` (3.0 ms, once in 4,000), as before.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with the numbers, the sign column, the git signs and the status line of the interface (UI-5, UI-9, UI-10; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a busy machine: load average 10 at the start and 9.6 at the end; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel`; the bench's directory is no repository, so git's answers are not in it, but the bar and the signs' lookup are drawn on every frame). The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 255 µs for motions, 190 µs for insert and 90 µs for the panel's keys; at 240x60: 648 µs, 504 µs and 266 µs (calm on the same run: 225, 167 and 54 µs; 467, 412 and 151 µs). The git diff and the status are in threads and never on a key's path. The longest key is a `%` (3.1 ms, once in 4,000), as before.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with the whole interface (the tab bar, indent guides, listchars, the colorcolumn, TODO badges, the scrollbar and the yank's flash on top of UI-2 to UI-10; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a busy machine: load average 13 at the start and 10 at the end; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel`). The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 268 µs for motions, 209 µs for insert and 120 µs for the panel's keys; at 240x60: 772 µs, 623 µs and 377 µs (calm on the same run: 231, 170 and 57 µs; 476, 420 and 156 µs). Against the run before these (UI-4 to UI-13 added) depth at 240x60 costs about 105 µs more in `Sim::update` (460 µs of mean against 355 µs) and 20 µs more in the Painter (114 against 93 µs): the walk of `layout::colors` makes glyphs, badges and columns for each char of the rows on screen, `editor` puts them in the rows, and the Painter merges more ranges. Shortening the scope's and the blank lines' search for their neighbors (300 and 50 lines to 120 and 30) changed nothing measurable, so they are as they were. Still under 1 ms at p99, with 230 µs to spare at the worst, on a machine at three times the load a quiet one has. The longest key is a `%` (3.7 ms, once in 4,000), as before. The bench's directory is no repository and has no search, so git's answers and the search's marks are not in it.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with the picker (PICK-1 to PICK-5) and the lit matches of a search (UI-14; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a machine at load average 16 at the start and 12 at the end, other jobs running; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,picker,lit`). The `picker` kind opens Ctrl-f's search of contents (over the 300 files and the 100,000-line one) and Ctrl-p's finder, types, moves, clears and closes them, and plays the shell after each key (the read of a preview, the highlighter's answer, the batches of results): each event is timed as a key is, since it is on the main thread too. The `lit` kind is `motions` after `/let`, with a match in nearly every line on screen. The p99 in depth at 80x24: 279 us for motions, 326 us for the picker and 317 us for lit; at 240x60: 762, 835 and 922 us (calm on the same run: 224, 265 and 229 us; 467, 558 and 476 us). The lit matches cost about 160 us at 240x60 in depth (a regex over the 60 lines on screen, found again for each frame), the picker about 70 us more than a key of the text: its window is composed and laid over the rows, and the preview's frame is made again only when the line, the size or the colors change. Before three cheaper paths (the width of a row cut by counting, ASCII cut by its length in `layout::fit`, the text on the accent's solid color found once) the picker took 895 us there; an earlier run at load 9.5 had 788 us for the picker and 855 us for lit. Under 1 ms, with 80 to 240 us of margin at the worst, on a machine at several times the load a quiet one has.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with the colors and Markdown's (COLOR-1, UI-8, UI-15: headings with a hue and bands, tables, task lists and thematic breaks drawn by glyphs, the hidden chars' sealed alert block, the whitespace marks, the fog's new floor and the secondary gray that does not fade; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a machine at load average about 10 when the run ended, other jobs running; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel,picker,lit`, one run). The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 268 us for motions, 194 us for insert, 110 us for the panel's keys, 308 us for the picker and 326 us for lit; at 240x60: 790, 620, 380, 828 and 862 us (calm on the same run: 225, 158, 51, 243 and 225 us at 80x24; 486, 430, 158, 536 and 498 us at 240x60). Against the run of the picker (same bench, load 12 to 16) depth at 240x60 is within noise for motions (790 against 762 us) and lower for lit (862 against 922 us; the load differed); the Painter's mean there is 123 us for motions (the fog's test of the secondary gray is one comparison per cell; the picker's run had 114 us). The longest key is a `%` (3.3 ms, once in 4,000), as before. Under 1 ms at p99 with 140 us of margin at the worst.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again after the AI-only features went (the proposals, REVIEW and its card, `:ref`; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a machine at load average about 10 and other jobs running, one run; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel,picker,lit`; the `review` kind of the bench is gone with REVIEW). Against the run before it (the colors and Markdown's looks) nothing moved beyond the noise of the load: the painter's mean in depth at 240x60 is 126 µs for motions, as it was. Under 1 ms at p99 with 147 µs of margin at the worst. SPEED-2 on the same machine, 100,000 files: 59 µs before the first frame in process, 7.5 ms to the first frame of the real binary under a pty, 39 ms for the list of files and 351 ms for git status, both in the background.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again as one program (the fold, 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a machine at load average 9 at the start and 12 at the end, one run; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel,picker,lit`). The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 269 µs for motions, 193 µs for insert, 114 µs for the panel's keys, 307 µs for the picker and 327 µs for lit; at 240x60: 791, 625, 390, 783 and 864 µs (calm on the same run: 228, 159, 53, 244 and 241 µs at 80x24; 494, 438, 154, 520 and 483 µs at 240x60). The mono Painter of COLOR-2 is not in the bench: it writes less than the colored one.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with which-key's window and the help (KEY-3, KEY-4; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a machine at load average 10 to 11 the whole run, other jobs running, one run; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel,picker,whichkey,help,lit`). Two kinds are new. `whichkey` types a prefix and plays the pause as an event of its own (`SimEvent::Pause`, timed as a key is, since it is on the main thread), so the window is drawn: `<Space>`, `<Space>b`, `d` (the longest list: motions and objects, cut to 16 rows), `di`, `yj`, `z`, `g`, `]`, `[`, and `<Space>bp` and `<Space>bc` with the letters on the tabs, the next key typed on with the window open and Esc. `help` opens the help with `:help` and with F1, types `tab`, `leader`, `dd` and `search` into it, moves in it, clears it and closes it (the table is filtered by the finder's fuzzy match on every key). The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 270 us for motions, 204 us for insert, 117 us for the panel's keys, 299 us for the picker, 221 us with which-key's window, 281 us in the help and 328 us for lit; at 240x60: 801, 628, 392, 787, 705, 926 and 868 us (calm on the same run: 227, 159, 56, 235, 181, 328 and 228 us at 80x24; 491, 435, 166, 547, 527, 722 and 481 us at 240x60). The worst p99 is the help's at 240x60 in depth, 926 us, under the 1 ms by 74 us; its slowest events are mostly the Enter of `:help` that opens it (about 0.97 ms in the run that printed them), which makes the picker's window and its first preview. The rows of the help (`keys::rows`) were made at every opening, 144 rows with their sequences expanded, and are made once now (`OnceLock`): that took the help's p99 at 240x60 in depth from 972 and 987 us (two runs) to 919 and 926 us (two runs), which is within the noise of the load. The `whichkey` kind is no slower than `motions` (705 against 801 us at 240x60 in depth): the window is a pass of `pick::lay` over at most 24 rows laid over the composed rows. The longest key is a `%` (3.6 ms, once in 4,000), as before. After the keys were written for people and which-key lost its shadow (a second run, `SPEED_1_ONLY=whichkey,help`, load 10): the p99 of which-key at 240x60 in depth is 654 us (705 before) and the help's 934 us (926 before), the same within the load's noise. SPEED-2 on the same machine, 100,000 files: 67 us before the first frame in process, 8.7 ms to the first frame of the real binary under a pty (1.3 ms for the wrapper alone, and the 3 ms wait for the terminal's colors is in it), 40 ms for the list of files and 341 ms for git status, both in the background.
  - **Decided** (P4): measured again with the changes view and the guarded git (DIFF-1, GIT-1; 2026-10-17, release build, AMD Ryzen 9 9950X, a machine at load average 13 rising to 20 during the first run and 19 falling to 11 during the second, other jobs running; `SPEED_1_ONLY=motions,insert,panel,picker,whichkey,help,lit,changes`, then `help,lit,changes`). The new kind `changes` makes a repository of the bench's files (committed, then a line added to each of the 300 small ones), has git's status and the view's numbers answered off the clock, opens the view with `<Space>ge`, and moves in it: lines, half pages, pages, to the top and the bottom, folding and unfolding a folder. Every key in it is a frame of 300 rows in 12 folders, each with its letter and numbers. The p99 of a key in depth at 80x24: 149 us in the first run and 154 us in the second; at 240x60 741 and 625 us (calm: 112 and 123 us; 345 and 380 us). A cut costs nothing a key notices: the rename check allocates only for a row that shows more than its file's name.
